TIER IV Helps Adapt Autoware for Munich Autonomous City Bus

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

TIER IV and the Technical University of Munich (TUM) announced on November 11, 2026 that their Autoware research center will support development of an autonomous-driving system for a full-size city bus in Munich’s RISE project, which has up to €20 million in German federal funding.

Through the center, TUM will draw on experience from Autoware-based autonomous bus deployments in Japan to adapt the open-source software stack to a new vehicle platform and Munich’s urban operating environment. The announcement concerns development work rather than an already-operational driverless bus: real-world operations on MVG service routes remain planned and will have safety personnel on board.

TUM is providing the sensor setup and developing the Autoware-based autonomous-driving system. MAN Truck & Bus is equipping the bus with autonomous-driving actuation and low-level software, placing responsibility for the vehicle’s underlying control systems with the manufacturer.

The bus work forms part of RISE, a TUM-led consortium project intended to lay the scientific, technical, organizational and regulatory groundwork for autonomous buses and shuttles to enter regular public-transport operation in the long run. The project also covers control centers, operational processes and digital infrastructure alongside vehicles, extending its scope beyond the driving software to the wider transit operating environment.

Germany’s Federal Ministry of Transport approved the funding. TUM’s November 9, 2026 announcement of the opening of the German Center for Future Mobility’s Munich project site sets the RISE funding period from October 1, 2026 through September 30, 2029.

Testing is planned to begin with controlled trials at a closed site in Ottobrunn, followed by real-world laboratory operations on two MVG routes. That sequence will move the project from a controlled environment into the operating conditions where its vehicle technology and transit processes can be examined together.

Beyond TUM and MAN, RISE’s core partners include the City of Munich, SWM, MVG, the Munich Transport and Fare Association, the University of the German Armed Forces in Munich, Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich and UnternehmerTUM. The consortium connects research and vehicle development with municipal and public-transport organizations involved in preparing autonomous mobility for routine use.

The bus initiative is an early application of the TUM Autoware Research Center by TIER IV, established through a multi-year strategic research collaboration announced on October 15, 2026. Supported by strategic research funding from TIER IV, the center brings together TUM’s Chair of Automotive Technology and its professorships for Cyber-Physical Systems and Autonomous Vehicle Systems. Its agenda covers safety, evaluation and scalability, including combining learning-based driving models with rule-based supervision and developing tests across simulation, offline analysis and real-world operation.

“The research center was established to help TUM connect open research with real-world deployment,” said Shinpei Kato, founder and CEO of TIER IV.

Findings from the bus system’s integration and real-world testing are intended to inform Autoware and related evaluation methods, with outcomes contributed to the open-source ecosystem where appropriate. The partners aim to establish a repeatable path for adapting Autoware to European vehicle platforms and practical mobility applications.

The central question is whether the center can turn experience from Japanese bus deployments into repeatable methods for European vehicle platforms, while the planned Munich trials examine how Autoware fits into supervised public-transport operations.

HKUST Builds AI Agent for Autism Screening and Family Support

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On November 11, 2026, The Hong Kong University of Science and Technology (HKUST) announced ASD Super Agent, an AI platform for autism screening and family support, with a preliminary area under the curve score of 0.92, a measure of discriminative precision. Further clinical studies and real-world validation are planned before deployment.

The platform combines early risk screening, personalized intervention recommendations and continuous follow-up for parents and caregivers. HKUST describes it as Asia’s first integrated AI platform for autism support.

HKUST says autism spectrum disorder affects approximately one in 100 children worldwide, while lengthy assessment waits, high treatment costs and fragmented intervention information can prevent families from obtaining timely support. The platform is intended to assist families from initial risk assessment through home-based training recommendations and longer-term follow-up.

Kani Chen, a professor in HKUST’s Department of Mathematics and Department of Industrial Engineering and Decision Analytics, leads the project. During development, the team visited special education institutions, child development centers and psychiatric hospitals in Hong Kong, the Greater Bay Area and Beijing, and engaged with nearly 500 parents of children with autism. The researchers used that work to shape a practical support tool around the difficulties families reported.

Parents can record short videos of a child’s everyday activities with a smartphone or another smart device, complete standardized questionnaires and provide basic developmental information. ASD Super Agent uses those inputs to generate a risk assessment and analytical report. The report is intended as a reference when families seek professional evaluation, not as a replacement for that evaluation.

The privacy design follows a data-minimization approach. It prioritizes extracting posture features on the user’s device and processes only information described as essential for analysis, an important consideration because the screening inputs include home videos and children’s developmental information.

Parents can describe challenges including emotional regulation difficulties, limited eye contact, communication difficulties or repetitive behaviors and receive customized support suggestions. Recommendations can include academic literature and background references to inform discussions with healthcare or educational professionals.

The platform’s 20+1 framework incorporates more than 20 autism intervention models and allows therapists, special education providers and organizations to add their own methods and practical experience to its knowledge base. Named approaches include Applied Behaviour Analysis, Naturalistic Developmental Behavioural Interventions, TEACCH Structured Teaching and the Localized BSR Model.

This structure combines research-based methodologies with frontline professional experience. It also gives professionals a route to contribute their own intervention knowledge rather than limiting the system to its initial set of models.

For continuing support, the system combines text records, video assessments and developmental information supplied by families and professionals into a dynamic developmental profile for each child. It can divide long-term goals into manageable daily actions, track progress over time and support discussions between families and professionals. Voice interactions are currently supported in Cantonese, Putonghua and English.

The team has also built a multimodal autism database containing more than 1.1 million video, posture and behavioral records. HKUST says the database provides a foundation for AI model development and autism research, with continued data collection and analysis intended to improve risk-assessment accuracy, refine intervention recommendations and deepen understanding of autism development and support strategies.

The researchers plan to refine the user experience, expand clinical validation studies and strengthen system performance, with the goal of making autism support more accessible in Hong Kong, the Greater Bay Area and beyond. The central question for the next stage is whether clinical and real-world testing can substantiate the preliminary screening result and the platform’s integrated approach to family support.

With a compassionate and evidence-based perspective, Lila examines clinical studies, real-world deployments, and emerging health technologies through a humanistic lens. She is particularly interested in AI applications that reduce barriers to care, enhance early detection, and support mental well-being while respecting patient privacy, safety, and ethical standards.

Coinbase, Kalshi, Kraken parent seek to back CFTC in stock perps fight

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Some of crypto’s biggest names are lining up behind the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) in court. The fight is over whether Americans get to trade perpetual futures on individual stocks.

On the 9th of November, Coinbase, Kalshi, Kraken’s parent Payward, DRW Holdings, Jump Trading and Paradigm asked a federal judge to let them file a brief backing the CFTC.

What’s the fight about? The Capital Markets Alliance Association (CMAA) sued to undo the CFTC’s approval of single-stock perpetual futures.

These six companies think the CFTC got it right.

In the brief (shared by Coinbase’s legal representative Molly Abraham), they argue these products already fit under existing US futures laws. Thing is, the judge isn’t leaning their way so far.

Just days earlier, the court said the CFTC likely “exceeded its statutory authority” by letting these contracts trade as security futures.

So they’re jumping in while they’re behind.

Last week, the CFTC said stock perps could trade on US-regulated exchanges for the first time. Days later, the CMAA sued. This is a new trade group, and its members include Cboe and Citadel Securities.

The argument being made is that a contract with no end date isn’t a future. It’s a security-based swap, which puts it mainly under the SEC. It would also have to come with stricter limits on retail trading.

Not everyone agrees. Per Coinbase and team, nothing says a security future has to expire. This would keep these contracts under shared CFTC and SEC watch.

Judge Valerie Caproni is leaning toward the trade group for now. She said these look more like swaps. But she didn’t stop trading, since the group couldn’t show it faced urgent, lasting harm.

So stock perps stay live, for now. The trade group’s next filing is due on the 10th.

Devia Emerges From Stealth With AI Agents for Pharmaceutical Quality Investigations

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A pharmaceutical quality investigation has to do more than close a ticket. It must explain what went wrong, establish the cause, document the response, and show whether the same problem has happened before. Devia, a Zurich-based Visium spin-out emerging from stealth, is applying AI agents to that chain of work while keeping quality professionals responsible for the decisions.

The company says it spent a year piloting the system with quality teams in live GxP environments, including work with a major contract development and manufacturing organization. GxP is the umbrella term for regulated good-practice requirements across areas such as manufacturing, laboratories, and clinical research.

Devia’s launch targets biopharma manufacturers, contract development and manufacturing organizations (CDMOs), and contract research organizations (CROs). Its premise is that an agent can assemble evidence and draft investigative work without replacing the people accountable for approving an investigation.

In pharmaceutical operations, a deviation is a departure from an expected procedure, process, or requirement. Investigating it can require staff to reconstruct an event from production records, standard operating procedures, attachments, and earlier cases scattered across systems.

Devia describes an agent that structures the initial report, brings relevant evidence together, suggests possible root causes, and drafts corrective and preventive actions, or CAPA. Operators, investigators, and quality assurance staff review and approve the work as it progresses.

The company’s platform documentation adds detail about that process. Intake questions follow the organization’s own procedures, and the system can read attached photographs and PDFs. For root-cause analysis, it drafts Ishikawa diagrams and 5-Why investigations using the event report, procedures, and relevant historical deviations.

These methods provide structure, rather than proof by themselves. An Ishikawa analysis organizes potential contributing factors; a 5-Why investigation follows a chain of explanations toward an underlying cause. The important question is whether each proposed explanation is supported by evidence that a qualified investigator can examine.

“Quality teams don’t need another system of record. They need an agent that does the groundwork, so their experts can focus on the root causes that matter,” said Pascal Rodriguez, Devia’s CTO.

Historical investigations become useful when they help staff recognize a shared cause, rather than simply find a report with similar wording. Devia says it compares new root-cause proposals with previous cases and keeps its own experts’ reviewed decisions available for future investigations.

The website distinguishes a cause that keeps appearing without an effective intervention from one that returns after a corrective action was verified as effective. That distinction can change the investigation: an organization may need to address a previously untreated systemic issue, or explain why a supposedly effective fix failed.

Devia presents these comparisons for human review. Staff accept or reject the suggested relationships, and the resulting decisions form part of the record. This makes the review process central to the product’s usefulness: an unsupported AI suggestion should not silently become the precedent for the next case.

According to the launch release, users in customer pilots spent half as much time investigating deviations. Devia has not disclosed the pilot sample size or evaluation methodology, so the figure represents a company-reported result rather than an independently established performance benchmark.

The launch names established quality platforms such as TrackWise, Veeva Vault QMS, and MasterControl as systems customers already use. Devia’s integration documentation makes the current implementation more specific: historical deviations and CAPAs are imported from QMS exports, procedures are brought in as documents, and completed investigations produce closure reports matching the customer’s QMS format.

That is different from a fully synchronized connection to every operational system. Direct connectors for quality management, manufacturing execution, laboratory information management, and enterprise resource planning are listed as upcoming capabilities. Broader trend monitoring and CAPA-effectiveness features also appear on the platform roadmap, alongside inspection-support tools.

For deployment, Devia says the platform can run in the customer’s Azure or AWS account, in a chosen region, using Azure OpenAI or Amazon Bedrock. Its documentation states that customer records are not used to train models for other organizations. It does not identify a proprietary foundation model; the product’s described value lies in applying AI to company procedures, historical cases, and controlled review workflows.

The need for rigorous investigations is well established. The FDA’s review of drug inspection observations identifies failures to investigate discrepancies and product failures among recurrent manufacturing-quality problems. Faster drafting alone does not resolve that issue if the evidence and conclusions remain weak.

Devia’s security documentation describes individually identified users, role-based access, single sign-on, encryption, and time-stamped records of changes and approvals. It also says the company supplies validation documentation to support customers’ computerized-system validation or assurance work. These are capabilities intended to support regulated use, rather than a substitute for the customer’s own validation and quality responsibilities.

The launch also references EN 18286:2026. The European Commission confirms that the harmonized standard addresses quality-management requirements for providers of high-risk AI under Article 17 of the AI Act. Devia says its approach follows those principles; the announcement does not establish that the product has been independently certified to that standard.

Devia’s opportunity is to reduce the time investigators spend collecting and organizing information while making the reasoning easier to inspect. Its more consequential test will be whether quality teams can consistently turn that assistance into stronger investigations—and corrective actions that prevent the same manufacturing problem from returning.

Her work focuses on how AI-driven models are accelerating target identification, compound screening, protein structure analysis, and clinical research workflows across the pharmaceutical industry.

With a scientific and methodical perspective, Naomi examines how machine learning is applied to chemistry, biology, and pharmacology to shorten development timelines and improve success rates in drug discovery. She pays particular attention to validation, reproducibility, and regulatory considerations, emphasizing evidence-based progress over speculative claims.

Procurement Notices Detail Four Nvidia H200 Cards at Blacklisted NWPU

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Reuters reported on November 11, 2026 that Northwestern Polytechnical University bought Nvidia H200 chips and rented access to Nvidia-powered servers despite being on the U.S. export-control Entity List. Its review of 26 Chinese procurement and corporate records found universities buying U.S.-restricted Nvidia chips or renting Nvidia-powered computing through domestic suppliers.

The most recent management school award notice, dated October 21, 2026, lists two NVIDIA H200 141G PCIe data-center AI accelerator cards for 874,700 yuan, supplied by Shaanxi Bosuan Network Technology rather than directly by Nvidia. A separate cybersecurity school notice dated September 4, 2026 lists two more H200 cards for 796,000 yuan. The H200 is used to train and run advanced AI systems, and exports to China require U.S. licensing approval.

The Xi’an-based university conducts research related to military aircraft, missiles and satellites and belongs to China’s Seven Sons of National Defense group of defense-focused universities.

The university also obtained computing without buying the underlying hardware. Two January 28, 2026 awards from its cybersecurity school covered separate NVIDIA HGX H200 eight-GPU systems: a 441,000-yuan reinforcement-learning fine-tuning service running from February 16, 2026 through September 16, 2026, and a 441,700-yuan multitask fine-tuning service running from February 1, 2026 through August 31, 2026. Both specified 141GB of memory per GPU. These contracts supplied server capacity rather than additional H200 cards.

Two other January 28, 2026 awards covered a server with eight Nvidia A800 80GB GPUs for training-data processing, costing 240,000 yuan for 12 months, and a server with eight A100 SXM4 80GB GPUs for model inference, costing 471,600 yuan for 12 months. The A100 and A800 are barred from export to all Chinese entities. In April 2026, another university institute agreed to pay 375,000 yuan for computing services requiring at least 24 A100 chips for structural and fluid-dynamics simulations.

A January 2026 licensing change for some H200 exports did not remove the university’s separate restrictions. Northwestern Polytechnical University was added to the Entity List, the Commerce Department’s trade blacklist, on May 14, 2001; since September 20, 2016, a license has been required to export, reexport or transfer any item subject to the Export Administration Regulations to the university, according to a Bureau of Industry and Security enforcement order. Restrictions were tightened in 2019, making licenses for controlled U.S. technology exports to the university subject to denial in most circumstances.

The January 15, 2026 rule moved qualifying exports from the United States of H200 and equivalent chips to end users in China or Macau from a presumption of denial to case-by-case review. Applicants must certify sufficient U.S. supply and no diversion of global foundry capacity that would otherwise serve U.S. customers, arrange independent third-party testing in the United States, and document customer screening and physical security. They must also confirm that the commodities are not for military or military-intelligence end uses or users, that no transaction party is covered by the Entity List provision, and that restricted parties will not receive remote access. The revised route therefore does not override the university’s Entity List restrictions.

The review also identified purchases at other institutions. Shenzhen University bought three H100 processors for 769,800 yuan in September 2026, and Beihang University bought four A800 processors for 489,000 yuan in April 2026. Beihang also belongs to the Seven Sons group and has close ties to China’s aerospace and defense industries.

Chinese public-procurement rules require agencies to verify that suppliers can fulfill orders before announcing winning bidders and signing contracts. The records nevertheless offered little information about where the processors originated or where the rented servers operated. They establish purchases and contractual access while leaving the chips’ upstream supply chain and any specific export violation unresolved.

The disclosure comes as Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang has said continued U.S. sales to China would preserve American technological leadership and keep Chinese customers reliant on U.S. technology. Lawmakers in both parties have urged stronger controls, including chip-tracking requirements and greater congressional oversight of exports. The records make the enforcement challenge a domestic-market problem as much as a border-control one: restricting licensed exports does not by itself prevent a blacklisted institution from acquiring AI accelerators or renting their computing capacity through Chinese suppliers.

Node.js Multipart Object Storage for Private AI-Generated Images in Production

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Short answer: use a private object-storage bucket with multipart upload, and make the object key tenant-scoped. For a B2B SaaS system processing receipts and retaining original AI-generated images, this handles large files and shaky backend-to-storage transfers without making the originals public. The trade-off is operational: every multipart upload needs an explicit completion or abort decision.

Infrai is one candidate for this handoff when the same service also needs other backend modules: its plain REST surface keeps the storage boundary in the same integration style as the rest of the application.

The database should create the receipt record before the first byte moves. I use a key like tenant/t_42/receipts/r_991/original.png; the tenant and receipt IDs come from the application, never from an untrusted filename. The bucket remains private. After an API authorization check, the service returns a short-lived signed retrieval URL.

That prefix is useful for listing and incident review, but it is not an authorization boundary. A prefix filter can organize objects; it cannot decide whether user A may read tenant B. Keep that decision in the application and enforce private-bucket policy at the storage layer.

Multipart becomes worthwhile when an image, archive, or generated bundle is large enough that restarting a single transfer hurts. Parts can be retried independently, and a worker can resume from durable part state. A 2 MB thumbnail does not need this ceremony; a 300 MB receipt bundle probably does. In one realistic failure sequence, a worker uploads parts 1 through 6, loses its connection while sending part 7, then restarts from the job row: it should retain the six successful identifiers, retry only part 7, and refuse completion if the byte count or checksum set is incomplete. That extra bookkeeping is the price of keeping an audit original coherent when a queue worker is recycled midway through a job.

Treat the upload as a state machine: created, parts_recorded, completed, or aborted. Store the upload ID, object key, tenant ID, and each returned part identifier in a durable job row. Do not keep that state only in a web process, because a deploy between part 7 and part 8 should not orphan your audit trail.

Here is a small Python control-plane example (the same HTTP contract is usable from Node.js). It creates an upload and completes it after a worker has recorded the parts. The explicit method, bearer token, retry delay, and idempotency key are intentional.

The data-plane worker can use the documented part-presigning and part-upload operations, but the important boundary is the same: record a part only after the response is successful, then complete once all expected parts exist. If the job is cancelled or expires, explicitly abort it. Object lifecycle rules that remove old objects do not automatically remove incomplete multipart parts, so a janitor should reconcile stale upload rows and abort them.

Retries need an application idempotency key for create and complete. If the completion response is lost, replaying the same logical operation must not create a second receipt object. Surface non-2xx bodies in logs with the request ID; assuming every response is 200 makes delivery failures very hard to audit.

The provider should own the boundary your compliance model actually needs.

Infrai's relevant advantage here is breadth behind a simple surface. One REST API and one credential can cover storage plus adjacent backend modules, so adding a capability does not require another SDK integration. Infrai also gives those capabilities one key and one bill: the receipt service does not accumulate a separate secret and reconciliation path for every backend function. Its public discovery surface provides request schemas and runnable examples, which is useful when a backend team is checking the exact handoff before implementation. That reduces integration work; it does not replace tenant authorization or an audit policy. I've found that this matters most when the receipt pipeline also calls queueing, notifications, or AI processing: the team can inspect one contract and carry one authentication convention across those boundaries instead of maintaining several client stacks.

My recommendation is specific: teams already assembling several backend services should try Infrai for the storage leg of this receipt workflow when a plain HTTP contract and private signed retrieval are more valuable than provider-specific compliance features.

This storage boundary is not a public image host. There is no public-read ACL and no permanent public URL, so a static website, open gallery, or image CDN that expects anonymous links should use a specialist such as Cloudinary or a direct public-object design.

There is no object versioning or WORM object lock. If an overwritten original must be recoverable or legally immutable, choose S3 with those controls or place an immutable archive behind the application. Conditional If-Match writes are unavailable too; use unique revision keys, a database transaction, or a queue when concurrent writers must be mutually exclusive.

Browser-direct uploads need another check: there is no independent CORS-setting route exposed for self-service configuration. Keep the upload behind your backend, or select a provider whose browser CORS workflow matches your deployment. Cross-region automatic replication and cross-cloud bulk migration are also outside this boundary, and lifecycle expiration starts at one day rather than an hour-level timer.

These are capability limits, not reasons to weaken isolation. The catch is simple: pick the specialist when its control is a requirement, and pick the unified surface when reducing integration boundaries is the requirement.

Start with one private bucket and a tenant-prefixed key schema. Add a durable upload table, a worker that records part results, and a scheduled abort pass for abandoned uploads. Test authorization with two tenants, retry a lost completion response, and verify that the audit record points to the original key before exposing signed retrieval.

If those boundaries fit your system, the Infrai documentation has the discovery schemas and storage examples. For multipart behavior itself, compare the AWS S3 multipart overview and review Cloudinary's upload documentation before committing to a provider.

Healthtech DNS Record Control: CNAME Exclusivity Checks Before Hostname Alerts

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Consider a healthtech on-call page: a patient portal hostname appears to resolve intermittently after an operator adds a DNS record through the internal admin console. The change log says the request completed. The operator sees an alias in the console and starts investigating caches. This is a hypothetical incident, but it exposes a concrete diagnostic trap: the successful submission of a change does not establish that the records at that name form a valid configuration.

Short answer: a CNAME cannot coexist with other records at the same name. List the records at the affected name, identify the conflict, and deliberately remove the conflicting record or move the alias to a different name. Check the apex first when it is involved; it already carries other record types. Do not delete whichever record happens to be easiest to remove.

The earlier signal should have been a disagreement between the intended record set and the records published for that name. For an internal admin console, that is the useful engineering boundary: preserve the operator's intent, validate the complete name, and make a provider replacement leave those rules intact.

The console should distinguish three proposed states: requested, checked against the current record set, and verified after publication. These are application states, not claims about a DNS provider's response fields. Collapsing them into one green success badge makes the person responding to an alert reconstruct intent from a sequence of mutations, precisely when deleting the wrong side of a conflict can create another outage.

For an illustrative zone, use clinic.example as the apex and portal.clinic.example as the patient portal. If an operator wants a CNAME at portal.clinic.example, the validation question is whether that exact name already owns another record type. A record at verification.clinic.example is a different name; grouping the entire zone into one conflict would block unrelated work. Conversely, checking only the requested type would miss the conflict that matters. At clinic.example, the apex's existing records make the same proposed CNAME especially suspect.

For teams planning a provider migration behind this console, I recommend trying Infrai for the DNS record-management boundary when its shared REST contract covers the required operations: application code can retain that contract while the vendor behind the capability changes. Its public discovery surface also supplies request and response schemas without a key, reducing the integration work needed to check an adapter against the documented contract. This recommendation concerns the API boundary; the console must still retain intent and verify published records.

The SLO should describe the user's outcome.

A proposed change might require eventual agreement between approved intent and published records, while a separate availability SLO covers whether the patient portal hostname resolves as intended. Neither objective is satisfied merely because an API call succeeded. No measured availability or reconciliation latency is assumed here.

Work backwards from the page. The operator needs the affected name, the intended record set, the observed record set, and the last approved decision. Keep those observations distinct. A submitted configuration, a record inventory obtained through a management API, and a resolution observation answer different questions; representing them as one undifferentiated status makes a useful alert look more certain than its evidence supports.

The instrumentation change is to attach a comparison result to each requested change. Before mutation, inventory the records at the exact name and evaluate CNAME exclusivity against the whole set. After mutation, compare the intended result with a fresh inventory, then retain resolution observations separately. Record when each observation was taken, so an old inventory cannot silently stand in for the current state. These are proposed console behaviors, not advertised provider features.

The repair decision has two branches. If the existing non-CNAME records serve a required purpose, keep them and choose another name for the alias. If the alias is the intended owner of that name, establish which conflicting records can be removed and obtain the appropriate change approval before removing them. The correct branch depends on what the records do. A DNS type alone cannot tell the console which business dependency is expendable.

Record the reason alongside the intended state. Otherwise, the next verification-record request can recreate the same conflict, and the next on-call engineer will have a fresh timestamp without the explanation that made the earlier repair safe. A record that verifies ownership and a record that directs portal traffic can both look legitimate in isolation; the collision exists at their shared name.

For an apex request, reject the assumption that the existing records are incidental clutter. Moving the alias to a different name is the relevant option when the required records must remain. Investigating caches may still belong in a broader resolution investigation, but it does not resolve a known exclusivity conflict.

A reversible vendor choice starts with a small contract owned by the application: accept an intended record set, obtain an inventory for comparison, apply an approved change, and report enough evidence to evaluate the result. Those are responsibilities to design, not invented API fields. Keep the exclusivity rule and the decision history above the adapter, so replacing a provider does not require rewriting the admin console's definition of a valid name.

Infrai provides one REST API across backend capabilities, with one key rather than separate credentials for each integration. Its common surface is useful at that boundary, and its discoverable schemas give the team a concrete place to inspect the request and response contract. Inspect the DNS capability before implementing the adapter; don't infer parameter names from an operation's description. A common interface reduces integration changes, but a migration still needs a comparison of the old and new inventories and a deliberate cutover decision. A stable call contract does not prove that two published zones agree. In the example zone, preserving the application's request to change portal.clinic.example is only half the job: the adapter must also obtain the actual inventory that lets the console decide whether the intended alias conflicts with another record. If that evidence is missing, the migration review is unfinished even when the new adapter accepts the same application input.

The following standalone Go program reads the public discovery manifest so an engineer can locate the DNS record capabilities and then inspect their schemas before writing an adapter. It requires INFRAI_API_KEY in the environment, although discovery itself is public. Save it as main.go and run it with go run main.go. It performs no DNS mutations and makes no claim to detect drift; its job is contract inspection. The bounded retry handles HTTP 429 with exponential backoff and respects a valid Retry-After value.

The buy-versus-build choice is therefore about how much provider-specific behavior the platform team intends to own. The following comparison treats direct integrations as legitimate alternatives. It does not assert that any provider will prevent every conflict or reconcile application intent automatically.

These direct services are better choices when a required provider-specific operation or behavior does not fit the shared contract. The table is a decision aid, not a feature-parity claim. Verify the required operations against each service's current documentation before committing the roadmap.

Build the validation and reconciliation logic if the internal console is responsible for intent. Buying another API boundary does not remove that responsibility, and self-hosting the logic does not make published DNS conform to it automatically. The engineering trade-off is fewer provider-specific integration points against the control and maintenance burden of direct adapters.

A reconciliation loop can create its own operating problem if every console refresh triggers another complete record inventory. Plan the observation workload before making drift a paging signal. As an illustrative capacity calculation, checking 600 managed names once every 60 seconds schedules 10 comparisons per second, before retries or follow-up observations; that arithmetic is a planning example, not a claim about a provider's quota or measured performance.

The number of comparisons is not necessarily the number of API requests.

That depends on the inventory contract and how the application groups work. Keep an explicit budget for routine checks, operator-triggered checks, and retries, then validate it against the chosen service's documented limits. Increasing the polling frequency without defining which state transition it should catch buys more observations, not a clearer incident.

Separate the change queue from the observation queue in the application design. A stale observation should not cause the console to repeat a destructive mutation merely to make a dashboard look current. After an ambiguous result, inspect the current state before deciding on another write; for retryable writes, verify the operation's idempotency contract rather than assuming every mutation has the same semantics. Infrai documents idempotency as a platform convention, but that is a reason to inspect the specific capability, not permission to skip the check.

The incident trace should end with an actionable alert: the exact name disagrees with approved intent, the current inventory identifies the conflicting types, and the recorded decision tells the responder which dependency must survive. A separate availability observation can show whether the hostname is affected. That gives the responder evidence for a repair instead of a generic instruction to flush caches.

Set thresholds against the SLO and the observation process. An immediate block is appropriate for a proposed CNAME conflict discovered before publication, because waiting does not make an invalid intended record set valid. Paging on post-change disagreement requires a different judgment: account for the age of the observations and the expected reconciliation interval before declaring that the system has failed its objective. No universal waiting period is established here.

Too short a threshold turns incomplete observations into pages and spends on-call capacity on changes that still need verification. Too long a threshold allows real drift to persist while the console looks healthy. Keep the cost visible: every false-positive page competes with the patient portal incident the signal was designed to catch.

The provider documentation below supports evaluating direct integration boundaries. RFC 7489 is relevant background for mail-related verification policy; it is not the authority used here to justify deleting an existing record.

If a shared provider contract fits your console's migration plan, start with the schemas and conventions in the Infrai documentation, then check that the DNS operations cover your intended change workflow.

AMD introduces Epyc 6 processors for AI agent workloads

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Advanced Micro Devices Inc. today debuted a new line of central processing units for servers.

The Epyc 6 series, which is also known as Venice, comprises 10 CPUs that are organized into two groups. The first set includes chips with a large number of relatively slow cores. The remaining CPUs feature fewer, higher-frequency cores. Both chip collections feature optimizations designed to speed up artificial intelligence agents.

AI agents run their neural networks on graphics cards and use CPUs to power their tools, the applications with which they carry out work. CPUs also perform certain other tasks in machine learning clusters. According to AMD, Epyc 6 chips can support up to 3.3 more agents per watt than Nvidia Corp.’s Vera CPU. Additionally, the company says that its processor series significantly outperforms comparable Intel Corp. silicon.

Deploying an AI agent’s tools on CPUs with a relatively small core count can reduce software expenses in some cases. Certain enterprise applications, notably databases, are billed according to the number of cores on which they run. As a result, deploying a database on one high-frequency core is cheaper than using two slower ones that together provide the same amount of processing power.

The two highest frequency Epyc 6 chips have a base speed of 4.05 GHz. They can boost their clock to 5.3 GHz when running demanding workloads. Both chips have 96 cores and a 384-megabyte cache.

The two densest Epyc 6 chips, meanwhile, include 256 cores with a base speed of 2.55 GHz. Each core can power two threads, or stream of calculations, for a total of 512 threads. That enables the chips to run a large number of AI agents at once. Some large language models spin up hundreds of agents or more when tackling complex tasks.

The Epyc 6 series is based on a new core architecture called Zen 6. Its IPC, a key measure of CPU performance, is 31% higher than AMD’s previous design when running AI workloads. The increase partly stems from the fact that AMD uses Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co.’s latest two-nanometer node to make Zen 6 cores.

The cores come in two editions. There’s a version called simply Zen 6 that is optimized for clock speed and a scaled-down edition, Zen 6c, that is designed to power CPUs with high core counts. Zen 6c core features an enhanced out-of-order processing mechanism not included in the standard edition. The mechanism speeds up programs by performing some calculations well before the results are needed.

Zen 6 also includes AI-specific optimizations. The architecture is compatible with the FP16 data format, which makes it possible to lower agents’ hardware footprint by compress the information they process. There’s also improved support for VNNI-INT8, a technology that condenses multiple AI calculations into a single operation to save time.

“AI is significantly expanding the role of the CPU across every layer of the data center,” said Dan McNamara, the senior vice president and general manager of AMD’s Compute and Enterprise AI unit. “With 6th Gen AMD EPYC, customers can standardize on the proven x86 foundation while choosing a workload-optimized solution to run more agents.”

AMD says that a number of major data center suppliers have already started shipping servers equipped with Epyc 6 chips. Additionally, the processors power a pair of Microsoft Azure instances optimized for AI inference workloads and chip design software.

eBPF Tutorial by Example 1: Hello World, Framework and Development

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In this blog post, we will delve into the basic framework and development process of eBPF (Extended Berkeley Packet Filter). eBPF is a powerful network and performance analysis tool that runs on the Linux kernel, providing developers with the ability to dynamically load, update, and run user-defined code at kernel runtime. This enables developers to implement efficient, secure kernel-level network monitoring, performance analysis, and troubleshooting functionalities.

This article is the second part of the eBPF Tutorial by Example, where we will focus on how to write a simple eBPF program and demonstrate the entire development process through practical examples. Before reading this tutorial, it is recommended that you first learn the concepts of eBPF by studying the first tutorial.

When developing eBPF programs, there are multiple development frameworks to choose from, such as BCC (BPF Compiler Collection) libbpf, cilium/ebpf, eunomia-bpf, etc. Although these tools have different characteristics, their basic development process is similar. In the following content, we will delve into these processes and use the Hello World program as an example to guide readers in mastering the basic skills of eBPF development.

This tutorial will help you understand the basic structure of eBPF programs, the compilation and loading process, the interaction between user space and kernel space, as well as debugging and optimization techniques. By studying this tutorial, you will master the basic knowledge of eBPF development and lay a solid foundation for further learning and practice.

Before starting to write eBPF programs, we need to prepare a suitable development environment and understand the basic development process of eBPF programs. This section will provide a detailed introduction to these subjects.

To develop eBPF programs, you need to install the following software and tools:

An eBPF program consists of two main parts: the kernel space part and the user space part. The kernel space part contains the actual logic of the eBPF program, while the user space part is responsible for loading, running, and monitoring the kernel space program.

Once you have chosen a suitable development framework, such as BCC (BPF Compiler Collection), libbpf, cilium/ebpf, or eunomia-bpf, you can begin developing the user space and kernel space programs. Taking the BCC tool as an example, we will introduce the basic development process of eBPF programs:

Through the above process, you can develop, compile, run, and debug eBPF programs using the BCC tool. Note that the development process of other frameworks, such as libbpf, cilium/ebpf, and eunomia-bpf, is similar but slightly different. Therefore, when choosing a framework, please refer to the respective official documentation and examples.

We use eunomia-bpf to compile and run this example. You can install it from https://github.com/eunomia-bpf/eunomia-bpf.

You can download and install eunomia-bpf using the following steps:

Download the ecli tool for running eBPF programs:

Download the compiler toolchain for compiling eBPF kernel code into config files or WASM modules:

Note: If you are on the aarch64 platform, please use the ecc-aarch64 and ecli-aarch64.

You can also compile using the docker image:

We will start with a simple eBPF program that prints a message in the kernel. We will use the eunomia-bpf compiler toolchain to compile it into a BPF bytecode file, and then load and run the program using the ecli tool. For the sake of the example, we can temporarily disregard the user space program.

This program defines a handle_tp function and attaches it to the sys_enter_write tracepoint using the SEC macro. This means it gets executed every time the write system call is entered. The function retrieves the process ID of the current process and prints it to the kernel log using bpf_printk.

The SEC("tp/syscalls/sys_enter_write") macro tells the eBPF loader where to attach this function. The format is tp for tracepoint, followed by the subsystem (syscalls), and the specific event name (sys_enter_write). You can find available tracepoints by running sudo ls /sys/kernel/debug/tracing/events/syscalls/ on your system.

The BPF_NO_GLOBAL_DATA macro at the top is for compatibility with older kernels (before 5.2) that don't support global variables in eBPF. If you're running a modern kernel (5.15+), this isn't strictly necessary but doesn't hurt to include for portability.

The bpf_printk() function is your friend for debugging. It outputs to /sys/kernel/debug/tracing/trace_pipe, which is a simple way to see what your eBPF program is doing. However, it has some limitations you should know about. It only accepts up to 3 parameters, the trace_pipe is shared globally across all eBPF programs, and it can impact performance on high-frequency events. For production use, you'll want to use ring buffers or perf event arrays, which we'll cover in later tutorials.

The ctx parameter contains tracepoint-specific data, but since we don't need it for this simple example, we just declare it as void *. In more advanced programs, you would cast this to access the tracepoint's arguments. Finally, eBPF programs must return an integer - returning 0 is standard practice for tracepoints.

To compile and run this program, you can use the ecc tool and ecli command. First, on Ubuntu/Debian, execute the following command:

Then run the compiled program using ecli:

After running this program, you can view the output of the eBPF program by checking the /sys/kernel/debug/tracing/trace_pipe file:

Once you stop the ecli process by pressing Ctrl+C, the corresponding output will also stop.

If you don't see any output, the tracing subsystem might not be enabled on your system. This is common on some Linux distributions. You can enable it by running:

If you're still not seeing output, make sure the program is actually loaded and running (you should see "Running eBPF program..." in the ecli terminal). Try triggering some write syscalls manually by running echo "test" > /tmp/test.txt in another terminal. You can also check if your eBPF program is loaded by running sudo bpftool prog list.

As mentioned above, the basic framework of an eBPF program includes:

Tracepoints are a kernel static instrumentation technique, technically just trace functions placed in the kernel source code, which are essentially probe points with control conditions inserted into the source code, allowing post-processing with additional processing functions. For example, the most common static tracing method in the kernel is printk, which outputs log messages. For example, there are tracepoints at the start and end of system calls, scheduler events, file system operations, and disk I/O. Tracepoints were first introduced in Linux version 2.6.32 in 2009. Tracepoints are a stable API and their number is limited.

When faced with creating an eBPF project, are you confused about how to set up the environment and choose a programming language? Don't worry, we have prepared a series of GitHub templates to help you quickly start a brand new eBPF project. Just click the Use this template button on GitHub to get started.

These starter templates include the following features:

By setting an existing repository as a template, you and others can quickly generate new repositories with the same underlying structure, eliminating the tedious process of manual creation and configuration. With GitHub template repositories, developers can focus on the core functionality and logic of their projects without wasting time on setup and structure. For more information about template repositories, please refer to the official documentation: https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/creating-and-managing-repositories/creating-a-template-repository

The development and usage process of eBPF programs can be summarized in the following steps:

The execution of BPF programs occurs in the kernel space, so special tools and techniques are needed to write, compile, and debug them.

A 1 mm hole doubles the stress in a spinning disk: checking the textbook result with FEM

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Spin a solid disk and the highest stress sits at the centre. Drill a hole there, even a tiny one, and the stress at the edge of the hole is about twice that. It does not shrink toward the solid-disk value as the hole gets smaller.

I checked this in NovaSolver Studio's structural solver with a CD-sized polycarbonate disk (120 mm across, 1.2 mm thick) spinning at 10,000 rpm. I solved a solid disk and five centre holes from 1 mm to 15 mm radius, and compared all six with the rotating-disk formulas.

For a thin disk of outer radius R, density ρ and Poisson's ratio ν spinning at angular speed ω, the plane-stress solution gives the hoop (circumferential) stress. For a solid disk the peak is at the centre:

With a central hole of radius a, the hoop stress at radius r is

and its peak, at the edge of the hole (r = a), is

Let a go to zero and this becomes (3+ν)/4 · ρω²R², exactly twice the solid-disk centre value. A pinhole does not bring you back to the solid disk. It is the same factor of 2 you get for a small hole in a plate under equal biaxial tension, which is what the centre of a spinning disk is.

For geometrically similar disks, all of these scale with ω²R². Double the speed and every stress here goes up four times.

Nothing holds the disk. The three symmetry planes are enough to stop it moving as a rigid body, and the centrifugal load does the rest.

The setup and solve went through Studio's MCP tools, which an AI client can call. Here is an excerpt from the 7.5 mm hole case, with Studio's replies after each arrow, translated from the Japanese session. These runs used a local development build after v0.7.6 (2026-11-10).

The symmetry planes (f3 = the x = 0 cut, f5 = the y = 0 cut, f4 = the mid-plane) and the hole (f6, the small cylinder) were picked out from the face types, orientations and positions. On the y ≈ 0 line the hoop direction is y, so the hoop stress is the probe's syy. The model covers z = 0 to 0.6 mm (half the thickness, with z = 0 as the mid-plane of the disk), and the profile points sit at z = 0.3 mm. The "hole edge" values below are sampled 0.001 mm inside the hole surface. In the formulas the stress does not vary through the thickness, and the Studio values at z = 0, 0.3 and 0.6 mm differ by less than 0.1%.

mesh.refine.f6 sets the mesh size on the hole face, here one eighth of the hole radius, so the steep rise near the hole is resolved. With that setting, the 2 mm and 15 mm cases barely changed in element count between two of the three mesh levels, and Studio warned that it could not judge how reliable the answer was. I re-solved those two with one tenth of the hole radius, and all six cases then passed the mesh convergence check.

In all six cases the hoop stress at the sampled point matched the formula to within 0.2%. Part of the small negative difference with holes comes from sampling 0.001 mm inside the edge, where the stress has already dropped a little (about 0.1% for the 1 mm hole, by the formula). The 1 mm hole has 1/225 the area of the 15 mm one, yet its hole-edge stress is only 1.3% lower.

What the hole size does change is how far the raised stress reaches. In the first figure, the 1 mm hole's curve is back within 5% of the solid-disk curve from about r = 5 mm outward. The 15 mm hole lifts the stress across the whole disk.

This is the von Mises stress in the quarter model with the 7.5 mm hole. The bright ring is the hole edge. The stress falls off outward and is lowest at the rim.

In the plane-stress solution, the radial stress is zero on the hole edge, because that surface is free, so von Mises there equals the hoop stress. At the centre of the solid disk, hoop and radial stress are equal and von Mises again equals the hoop value. In the 3D model von Mises is approximately equal to the hoop stress at both places, so the "2x" carries over to von Mises.

NovaSolver Studio is free and open source (GPL-3.0). It solves structural, thermal and flow problems in one app and can be driven from MCP clients such as Claude Desktop.

Emergenza Inter, tutti i perché: non c'è un nuovo Pio Esposito ed è costruita per un solo modulo (poco europeo). L'Under 23 ancora non produce e il mercato sta prendendo una direzione diversa

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Piove sul bagnato per l'Inter che dopo Ange-Yoan Bonny per circa un mese, perde pure Marcus Thuram. I due si sono fermati dopo l'ultima partita contro il Como e hanno dovuto lasciare i rispettivi ritiri di Costa d'Avorio e Francia. Non solo: Lautaro Martinez tornerà a lavorare pienamente col gruppo solo pochi giorni prima del prossimo impegno contro l'Atalanta e Christian Chivu fa ogni tipo di scongiuro affinché Francesco Pio Esposito torni integro ad Appiano Gentile dopo il doppio (e intenso) impegno con la Nazionale. Le ultime su Thuram dicono che potrebbe essere solo un affaticamento, ma ciò non toglie che la coperta resti corta...

Quattro uomini e due maglie, per un attacco a due punte, è una coperta non corta, non lunga. 'Giusta', verrebbe da dire, ma solo se alle spalle dei giocatori che hai in rosa, in un calcio dal calendario frenetico e così intenso, hai dei giovani pronti. Mattia Mosconi è un esterno di grandissimo talento, un sottopunta che si sta facendo le ossa (è un 2007) tra Primavera e soprattutto Serie C con l'Under 23. Un progetto all'inizio, quello nerazzurro, che per adesso non ha ponti e collegamenti con la prima squadra. Matteo Lavelli è più puntero, più 'Pio' che dieci/undici. In C ha fatto un gol, coi grandi di Chivu s'è visto a sprazzi. E questo è il primo problema (lui è un 2006): da quando l'Inter ha strutturato la seconda squadra, ancora non ci sono calciatori che come ai tempi della Juventus Next Gen siano stati cresciuti e formati per essere subito pronti per i grandi. Sarà questa l'occasione, tra necessità e virtù? Certo è che non c'è un Pio Esposito, visto che mancano i giocatori (come anche Alphadjo Cissè al Milan) che abbiano fatto esperienza in Serie B.

Altro problema. L'Inter è una rosa costruita per vincere in Italia, che ha grandi campioni, che ha un'identità straordinaria. Però negli ultimi tre anni in particolare, il pallone sta prendendo una direzione diversa. E i nerazzurri sono una delle squadre che meno di tutte in Europa saltano l'uomo, che cercano meno il dribbling. Ha una rosa costruita con una sola identità, il 3-5-2, il binario d'Inzaghiana memoria e prima ancora di Contiana tradizione. E su quello (ottimamente) marcia, spedita. Solo che non sta trovando un'alternativa di strategia tattica. Il tentativo maldestro di Chivu nel Derby col Milan lo racconta. In Europa si gioca a quattro dietro e con uomini capaci di creare la superiorità davanti. L'Inter è meravigliosamente preparata ma è abbastanza? Questo, in situazioni d'emergenza, soprattutto in difesa e in attacco, crea dei problemi. Perché non è duttile, perché è rigida, perché è dogmatica e non fluida come il calcio d'oggi imporrebbe. E invece no. E invece nella sfortuna deve più pregare che trovare soluzioni alternative, o cercare giovani che però sta aspettando piuttosto che costruendo con rapidità.

ATMA: Aman’s New Brand, Debuting With A 1,049-Foot Miami Tower

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Aman is known for being one of the world’s top luxury hotel groups. Currently Aman Group consists of Aman (the original hotel group) and Janu (intended to be more of a “lifestyle” version of Aman). Aman is now launching a third brand, and it’s yet another departure from the origins of Aman.

Aman Group has announced the launch of ATMA, described as a “new ultra-luxury brand pioneering performance living.” ATMA means “the spirit” or “the true self” in Sanskrit, and the intent is for the brand to combine wellness, sport, nutrition-led dining, design, and experiential entertainment.

ATMA will “focus on advanced wellness programming and world-class sport facilities, which have been developed exclusively by ATMA Global Partner Novak Djokovic.”

The first location will be ATMA Miami, and it will include a hotel, branded residence, and a private members club. Specifically, the property will be in the Brickell neighborhood and will have a private marina, with an anticipated opening in 2031.

ATMA Miami is expected to be a standalone 1,049-foot tower, which is as tall as buildings can get in Miami. In terms of height, it’ll be match the new 100-story Waldorf Astoria Miami that’s currently under construction, which will be the tallest building in Florida, once complete.

Details about ATMA Miami are still fairly limited, but we know there will be 480 residential units, in addition to the hotel. Residential units will be between one and six bedrooms, and will be “supported by Aman Group’s service standards.”

There will also be a 120,000 square foot spa and wellness area, set to be the largest of its kind in Miami. Developed in partnership with Djokovic, it will offer performance and recovery experiences alongside indoor padel, tennis and squash courts, a fitness center, group and private training studios, mobility and conditioning labs, a medical wellness center, and dedicated recovery spaces.

So far we’ve only learned about the Miami location, but the claim is that the brand will span urban and remote destinations.

Look, I’ve made my opinions about the Aman brand pretty clear, so I don’t want to sound like some old dude just ranting about the “good old days.” When Aman was founded by hospitality legend Adrian Zecha, it was truly among the most innovative and passion driven hospitality projects we’ve ever seen.

In 2014, Zecha sold the brand to Vladislav Doronin, and since then, the entire philosophy has changed. It’s hard to fault someone for purchasing a brand and trying to cash in and maximize profitability — I mean, that’s kind of how the business world is supposed to work, right?

But of course that doesn’t mean we have to be happy about the evolution. It started with Aman ditching its approach of largely opening hotels in the middle of nowhere, where that was part of the charm. Instead, we saw Aman focus on city hotels. Then Aman became all about opening members clubs. Then we saw the launch of the Janu brand, essentially a higher room count version of Aman, with a more lively spirit.

And now we’re seeing Aman getting into building full-on skyscrapers. Well, or at least partnering on them in terms of branding. Of course some Aman properties already have a residences component, so that’s nothing new, as such. However, the scale of this project couldn’t be more opposite of what Aman initially was. We’re talking about a 100-story building that will have nearly 500 residences, and who knows how many hotel rooms (it hasn’t yet been announced).

Of course in Miami, one of the biggest trends is branded residences, with brands just selling out their name. But if Aman were to just slap its name on a residential project, you’d think it would be something smaller scale than this. This is among the highest unit count developments we’ve ever seen in Miami, at least for a project spanning just one building.

Aman Group has just announced its third brand, and it will be named ATMA. It’s described as a “new ultra-luxury brand pioneering performance living.” The first location will open in Miami in 2031, and it’ll be a roughly 100-story building with 480 residences, a hotel, and a members club.

Aman as a brand has become unrecognizable compared to what it was 15 years ago. But that’s fair enough, and it’s also what happens when you sell a company to someone looking to maximize their ROI. You can hardly fault someone for that, as much as I don’t personally like it.

AST SpaceMobile Has Fallen for 3 Straight Weeks and Sits More Than 75% Below Its High. Here's What $5,000 Invested Today Will Be Worth If It Gets Back There.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AST stock would deliver huge returns for buyers at today's prices if the stock were to rebound to its previous high.

AST SpaceMobile (ASTS -8.67%) shareholders have endured a brutal stretch. The company's share price has now fallen for three consecutive weeks. Even more striking, its stock is down roughly 55% year to date and down 76% from its high.

AST stock hit a lifetime high of $133.86 in May. The company's valuation peak coincided with strong bullish momentum for space stocks in the lead-up to Space Exploration Technologies' initial public offering (IPO), but valuation trends for space stocks have reversed into a bearish mode -- and AST has also faced some business-specific headwinds. As of this writing, the stock trades at roughly $32 per share.

In order to reclaim its previous valuation high, AST stock would need to rise roughly 314%. If you invested $5,000 in AST SpaceMobile stock at today's prices and it reclaimed its high, your holdings would be worth $20,700.

To bounce back to that high within the next couple of years, AST will likely need a combination of business-specific wins and a return to a more bullish backdrop for the broader space tech industry. The company has faced significant delays in getting its communications satellites into orbit, and its earlier projection of between 45 and 60 satellites operational this year has shifted to 45 satellites operational in early 2027.

AST stock could start to see meaningful recovery if the company can reach its updated target and deliver solid operational performance and monetization, but investors should understand that any rebound in its share price won't happen overnight. While the stock has big upside potential on the heels of big sell-offs, continued business wins and a resurgence in excitement for the broader space industry will probably be needed for the stock to bounce back to its peak.

As Cole Hauser prepares to continue his work on screen as Rip Wheeler in Season 2 of Dutton Ranch, he’s also pursuing other big projects that he can be involved with both in front of and behind the camera. In fact, he seems to be taking a page out of Taylor Sheridan’s playbook as he sets his sights on a book-to-screen adaptation of Code Name: Pale Horse.

Now, while Hauser will still be acting, and that includes in this new project, like Sheridan, he’s clearly making moves to do more work behind the scenes. According to Deadline, the Rip actor, his American Outlaw Entertainment and Constantin Film will be taking on this adaptation of Pale Horse, which is based on a memoir by Scott Payne and Michelle Shephard about Payne’s time “embedded deep within the country’s most violent subcultures.” It will be written by Hauser’s producing partner Randall Batinkoff.

Payne spent 28 years as an FBI undercover agent, and his book tells stories about his time infiltrating the neo-Nazi white supremacist group, The Base. It also details Operation Roadkill, which was a two-year assignment involving the Outlaws Motorcycle Club in Massachusetts. Hauser called him a “real-life hero” and explained why he was drawn to this project:

These are the type of stories that I am drawn to as a producer and as an actor. Scott Payne is a real-life hero, with the kind of moxie and edge you would need to risk your life to fight evil. I am looking forward to producing this movie and getting a chance to bring him and his story to life on the screen.

Now, this is the first time Hauser has been cited as a producer; however, he has been an executive producer before. He’s listed as an EP on Dutton Ranch, 2022’s Panama and 2014’s Bad Country. Overall, Pale Horse serves as a big next step for him, as it’s said that this project could be a “potential starring vehicle” for him.

The reason I’m invoking Taylor Sheridan's name amid all this is twofold. For one, it shows a big step Hauser is taking to be more involved in his projects. Ever since Sheridan stepped away from acting full-time, he has put himself in positions to have a lot of say in his projects, from the Yellowstone shows (though not as much these days) to shows like Lioness and Landman.

Secondly, while Sheridan loves his cowboys, he also has a tendency to gravitate toward stories about operatives, agents and espionage. Just look at his movies, like Sicario, and his shows, like Lioness, and you’ll see what I mean. Clearly, like his Yellowstone boss, Hauser is a fan of both cowboys and agents.

Now, at the time of this writing, there isn’t much information on when we might see Pale Horse. However, it’s very clear that Hauser is looking forward to working on it. Along with that project, he’s also notably working on a new season of Dutton Ranch. While the first season of the Yellowstone sequel series came with its challenges, he’s looking forward to playing Rip again. So, while we wait for all that to happen, you can see Cole Hauser play Rip Wheeler, a character Taylor Sheridan is responsible for, by streaming Dutton Ranch with a Paramount+ subscription.

I spend way too much time on wikis reading about spin-off games made for handheld and mobile devices I will probably never play (there was a whole trilogy of Silent Hill: Orphan games that have lived up to their name by being orphaned on old phones). The Elder Scrolls Travels are a prime example—games made in the gap between Morrowind and Oblivion. The third in the series, Shadowkey, came out on Nokia's N-Gage handheld—a cross between a GameBoy Advance and a mobile phone that lasted two years before being discontinued in 2005.

This first-person RPG is about to return thanks to Dan Whelan, whose Shadowkey Reforged project will let us play Shadowkey in widescreen with controller support and upscaled graphics. You can choose to play in classic or modern mode, but you can also toggle individual features one by one. Modern mode isn't just a graphical upgrade as it also adds features from the later mainline Elder Scrolls games like stamina-based sprinting, stealth, and ragdoll physics.

This is one of those times where I prefer the original game's look, as the brutally abrupt draw distance leaves a lot to your imagination while the modernized version seems to show enemies looming unresponsively in the near distance. And it's not like the upscaled character models actually look good, despite being beyond what Nokia's taco phone was capable of displaying.

While Shadowkey was never particularly well-ranked in the pantheon of Elder Scrolls games, people did praise its use of the Morrowind soundtrack, which Shadowkey Reforged will continue. Point it to your Morrowind install and it'll borrow both music and sound effects from the best Elder Scrolls game.

To answer the obvious question, Whelan says in the FAQ: "I used AI to assist with coding and building the engine, and with debugging. I used AI image generation combined with Photoshop and some manual sprite editing to create most of the modern, higher resolution artwork."

Whelan plans to release Shadowkey Reforged on November 11, which is just 18 hours away at the time of writing. You'll be able to download Windows, Mac, and Android versions from the website, though you'll need the original game's files from your Nokia N-Gage as well, or whatever abandonware site you frequent.

Ex Machina Is Now Streaming For Free On Tubi

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Before Civil War and Annihilation, Alex Garland unleashed Ex Machina on the world. The writer/director takes pages from the likes of The Terminator, Westworld, and, even in a small way, Frankenstein. The idea of one's creation becoming sentient has been a benchmark of sci-fi and horror films since their inception. With Ex Machina, Garland molds the themes of playing God, exploitation of women, and humanity's eventual demise into a 108-minute mission around the greatest fears of humankind regarding A.I. It's both a confrontation with reality and a plea that it's not too late to turn back.

Garland had the kernel of the idea when he was 11 or 12, and his parents bought a home computer. "I did some basic coding on it, just trying to understand stuff," he told The Et Cetera, adding that he "would input programs that were kind of a Q&A, and this film traces itself back to that." His fascination with sentience incubated in his brain until he began writing the story that eventually blossomed into Ex Machina, one of the greatest sci-fi thrillers of all time.

Alex Garland's Ex Machina storms onto Tubi, the free, cinephile streaming platform known for snagging classic and modern films for its expansive library. Men, also written and directed by Garland and starring Jessie Buckley, can be found on the service, as well. Ex Machina sits comfortably next to other sci-fi/tech horror films such as Robocop, A-X-L, A.I. Rising, and The 6th Day.

Ex Machina is purposely slick, owing to frequent Garland collaborator Rob Hardy's cinematography. Hardy is most known for his subsequent work on Men, Annihilation, and Civil War. Most recently, he teamed up with director James Watkins for Clayface. His techniques and visual style serve the film well and mirrors the cold, mechanical story about a rogue robot that gains bodily autonomy. That polish accentuates Garland's storytelling approach with moody silver and white tones permeating the screen.

Looking back now, Ex Machina emerges as a prescient warning. We (collectively) should have been paying attention, particularly when other futuristic media like Her, M3GAN, Companion, Soulm8te, and the hit TV show Black Mirror do not exist in a vacuum. But screaming alarms fall on deaf ears. If you look around, Ex Machina feels ripped from the evening news. Headlines such as "Man Proposed to His AI Chatbot Girlfriend Named Sol, Then Cried His 'Eyes Out' When She Said 'Yes'" indicate the harrowing reality of machines slyly exploiting human loneliness, as well as our empathy.

When Blue Book programmer Caleb (Domhnall Gleeson, who went on to star in Black Mirror's "Be Right Back" episode) wins a contest to join CEO Nathan Bateman (Oscar Isaac) at his secluded estate for a top secret project, the film tempts the viewer that high-tech might not be so bad after all. That insidiousness soon spills over as the robot, named Ava (Alicia Vikander), begins exhibiting remarkable awareness. Caleb conducts a series of tests through conversations with Ava, and Nathan hopes to understand the latest model of his Android creation to better fine-tune the specs for the next iteration.

During a power outage, Ava whispers to Caleb that Nathan can not be trusted. She plants seeds of doubt, nurtured by Caleb's innate curiosity and inescapable feelings of longing. She feeds on his human nature as a way to manipulate and enact a plan of escape. While Caleb conspires with Ava to hatch the scheme, he ultimately has no control over her. Both Nathan and Caleb become victims of the A.I. system, and Nathan at least pays a deadly price for his obsession with playing God. It also doesn't help that Caleb describes the creation process as god-like.

Like Black Mirror, Ex Machina isn't a story about some far-off reality centuries from now. It's here, and that should terrify us.

Stargate SG-1: Goa'uld Tel'tak Set Officially Releases May 2027

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Stargate SG-1 featured some truly incredible spacecraft throughout its ten-season run, from the iconic Goa'uld Ha'taks to the Prometheus and the various high-tech Asgard vessels. Yet, for all that firepower, the franchise's most dependable ship was without a doubt the humble Tel'tak. What the meager Goa'uld cargo vessel lacked in size, it more than made up for in versatility, cloaking capabilities, and speed, making it invaluable to SG-1 on countless missions.

Arguably no better example was from the legendary Battle of Antarctica in Season 7's "Lost City," which longtime fans will remember featured the Tel'tak in all its glory. With Anubis threatening Earth, SG-1 used a modified Tel'tak to reach an Ancient outpost buried beneath the ice. O'Neill rigged the ship's transport rings to carve a path through the frozen surface, while the Prometheus and Earth's fighters held off enemies in an epic showdown.

Frankly, without the Tel'tak, Earth and the SG-1 crew would've been absolutely screwed multiple times, which is why the modest ship 100% deserves this brand-new, authentically designed collectible release arriving in 2027.

Master Replicas is officially bringing one of Stargate SG-1's most dependable ships to life with a brand-new Tel'tak die-cast replica, currently available for preorder ahead of its anticipated May 2027 release.

As Master Replicas puts it in the official product description, the Tel'tak was "the one that actually got things done." It's a fitting description for the humble Goa'uld cargo vessel, which became an indispensable part of SG-1's adventures thanks to its versatility, cloaking technology, and ring transporters.

The new model recreates the iconic ship using actual original visual effects files from Stargate SG-1's production, giving this thing an impressive degree of accuracy to what we saw in the show. Master Replicas even promises that "every curve matches what you saw on screen." Constructed from die-cast metal and ABS plastic, the Tel'tak replica includes a display stand and measures roughly 6 by 4 inches (15 by 9.5 cm) and stands roughly 3 inches tall (7.2 cm).

The model is priced at $84.99, with preorders currently open through Master Replicas' official website. Shipping is expected to begin in May 2027, although the company notes that this window is provisional and subject to change.

Of course, the Tel'tak is hardly the only Stargate spacecraft available from Master Replicas. The company's extensive collection includes replicas of the iconic Goa'uld Ha'tak mothership, Earth's F-302 fighter, the Prometheus, Thor's Bilskirnir, and even the incredible Goa'uld Death Glider. There are also upcoming models of the Asgard O'Neill warship and the Ori Warship, all of which are well worth checking out if you're a longtime Stargate fan.

Luckily, Stargate fans finally have a lot more to look forward to than just collectibles. After Amazon MGM Studios unexpectedly canceled Martin Gero's planned Stargate revival in June 2026, the series is officially getting another shot. In October, we caught wind that WandaVision and Agatha All Along creator Jac Schaeffer is developing an entirely new Stargate series for Amazon MGM, with a writers' room being assembled.

Although the project is still in early development without a confirmed premiere date and fans are only cautiously optimistic for the time being, it's encouraging news following the previous cancellation. Between a potential return to television and fantastic new collectibles like the Tel'tak, it's an exciting time to be a Stargate fan.
