How SCSKiller is trying to fix shader stuttering in PC games

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's difficult to state just how much I hate shader stutter, even in the best PC games. I'm probably the world's biggest arachnophobe and absolutely loathe mushrooms, yet I'd rather have a trained tarantula serve me deep-fried shiitake than endure the infamously stutter-heavy Star Wars Jedi: Survivor. At least, that was until I downloaded Shader Compilation Stutter Killer (SCSKiller).

This free app promises to heavily reduce or even completely get rid of shader compilation in modern PC titles. But can this open-source software actually deliver? I've tested it out with a handful of games that are notorious for stuttering, and when it's working at its best, SCSKiller is ingenious. Are you a frame-obsessed PC gamer? You need to download this app ASAP, and here's why.

Before explaining why SCSKiller is great, let's address what shader stutter actually is. In short, it's a brief visual hiccup that looks like a frame rate drop to the untrained eye. Why is this a PC-specific issue and not a console one? Because PC development isn't based on fixed hardware, whereas studios can pre-compile shaders focused on exact PS5 or Xbox Series X specs pre-release.

While frame dips and shader stutters can occur at the same time (here's how to improve FPS for better gaming), they're actually separate issues. Frame drops normally happen because of hardware-related bottlenecks; where either the GPU or CPU can't render certain visual elements or physics tasks quickly enough.

Shader stuttering is different. These little programs tell your GPU how to draw various in-game effects, like shadows, reflections or lighting. In many modern PC titles — especially Unreal 5 Engine games — shader stuttering occurs because the rendering pipeline is having to produce effects in real-time. As the next frame waits for the shader to compile, a brief frame spike may easily occur. Once you're aware of them, in-game stutters can be so distracting that they ruin immersion.

Common variety stutters can sometimes be largely overcome if you own a VRR display. Variable Refresh Rate screens (like NVIDIA G-Sync and AMD FreeSync models) dynamically match the refresh rate of your panel to the number of frames your GPU is currently drawing, substantially smoothing out visual performance. VRR displays can also help fix screen tearing in PC and console games.

CPU-based compilation stutters can't be fixed by screen or GPU tech. In these cases, stuttering can last for a couple of seconds, dropping in-game action to 0 fps, thus completely breaking gameplay. This happens because your PC is seeing a particular effect for the very first time. That's why compilation stutter is at its worst when initially entering new areas.

SCSKiller can vastly reduce stutter in the PC games I've tested it with, but it can't fix all stuttering. Instead, this app has been designed specifically to target and eliminate shader compilation stutter. In that regard, it's hugely successful.

I picked out a few games on my rig that are infamous for compilation stutter. In all but one instance, SCSKiller slayed this horrible PC defect. Once you install it, the tool automatically reads your game libraries, instantly flagging the titles that can benefit most under a "Recommended to compile" list.

By pre-compiling shaders into my graphics card's driver cache before I launched each title, I found compilation stutter was more or less eradicated in Silent Hill f, Silent Hill Townfall, Elden Ring and The Witcher 3: Wild Hunt — Remastered. Before I discovered SSCKiller, I found both Townfall and Wild Hunt to be unplayable due to shader comp stutter. For context, I'm super sensitive to frame spikes. After I let this app pre-compile the shaders for both, the ruinous hitches I experienced before disappeared. SSCKiller is a complete game-changer in the titles I've just mentioned.

My results from Silent Hill f have proven equally impressive. Before I pre-compiled the Japanese spookster's shaders, SSCKiller's advanced stats informed me my previous 10-minute session had been blighted with precisely 25 compilation stutters (highest being 246 ms), 67 shader hitches, with a 1 percent low of just 3 fps. After using the app, comp stuttering went down to zero, with my G-Sync monitor making any smaller hitches incredibly difficult to spot. As I said, total game-changer.

The pros are abundant and obvious; the cons relatively few. SSCKiller is free, intuitive to use, boasts handy before and after compilation stats, and that instant library-reading feature I mentioned means you don't have to go about digging for specific game inventories. When SSCKiller's mojo is cooking, it comprehensively fixes this loathsome defect.

The app is refreshingly upfront about its current limitations. As I type this, most of the games I have installed on my custom-built rig show as unsupported via the in-software filter. There's a handy "Why?" button that explains this limitation. At time of writing, a lot of titles throw up a "Not supported yet" error, explaining "shaders are compressed or packed: needs an engine reader".

Thankfully, you can get around this fairly easily via the built-in recorder feature. Simply click the "Record while I play" slider located in the bottom right corner of the app after you select a game and SCSKiller can analyze the rendering pipeline and pre-configure shaders after 10 minutes or so of gameplay. Be warned though: Using the app can trigger bans when playing games with anti-cheat software, so make sure you set Elden Ring to launch offline when using this tool.

Pre-compilation cycles can take a while, too. Though most of the games I configured only took a few minutes to process, even my mighty AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D needed 17 minutes to compile Hogwarts Legacy's 291,688 shaders.

On that note, SSCKiller can't fix Hogwarts. Even on my NVIDIA RTX 5090 rig, Portkey Games wizarding open-world has always been a horribly optimized port. It threw up six major compilation hitches during a 10-minute run despite the app's best efforts. For games blighted with traversal stutter and CPU spikes (like Hogwarts and 2023's Dead Space remake), unless you use a VRR display, stuttering will remain visible. For a gratis piece of software though, SSCKiller does one heck of a job at smoothing out compilation stutter.

Why Websites Show the Wrong Server Location When You Use a VPN

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There are many reasons why someone might use a VPN, but most often, it’s to conceal your real geographic location or prevent websites from tracking your online activity. It can also be a way to get around location-based restrictions that stop you from accessing certain websites or platforms.

When you connect to a VPN server in the United States, websites are supposed to detect that your online traffic is originating from the U.S. That way, you can access online platforms that use IP filters to block non-U.S. traffic or circumvent regional censorship when travelling outside the U.S. But if you connect to a VPN server in the United States while you’re vacationing in Mexico, and a website thinks your IP address is from Brazil, that might not solve your problem and may even add unnecessary complications.

If you’ve encountered something similar with your VPN provider, there are several fixes you can try. Depending on why your IP location is being misinterpreted, one of these should help you access the internet from the server location of your choice.

IP geolocation isn’t infallible. Websites rely on third-party IP databases to look up your location based on your current IP address. Maxmind, which has one of the largest such databases in existence, claims that its GeoIP products have a 99.8% accuracy. Lesser-known providers with smaller IP databases may be far less accurate.

Websites can also use more than just your IP address to estimate your location. For example, they can send WebRTC requests to your browser or directly query your device’s GPS. Moreover, VPN providers often have physical servers in one country while their IP addresses are registered to another country, and this discrepancy can also create issues. Let’s take a closer look at each of these problems individually, so you can find the fix that works best for you.

WebRTC is an open-source technology that lets your browser reduce latency when engaged in high-bandwidth tasks like streaming or video calls. Based on the IETF standard RFC 8828, it lets a website choose between your VPN server and your real IP address so it can establish the quickest path to transmit large amounts of data and reduce wait times or buffering.

But the catch with WebRTC is that it exposes both your VPN server and ISP-assigned addresses to websites, which can reveal your real IP location and cause websites to reject your VPN server country. Firefox now comes with built-in protections to prevent WebRTC leaks, while Chrome and Edge support third-party extensions like WebRTC Control that you can use to eliminate these issues. To check if your browser is leaking your real IP address during WebRTC requests, you can use a free tool like BrowserLeaks.

Even when a browser isn’t leaking your real IP address, DNS resolvers may fetch inaccurate location information if their nearest servers are too far from your current location. For example, a user in São Paulo may be geolocated to Chile when using Google Public DNS because that’s where Google’s nearest resolvers are located.

If you toggle on DNS leak protection in your VPN app, this will force websites to use your VPN’s resolver to perform DNS lookups. That often solves the problem, but, again, you can confirm the fix worked through a service like BrowserLeaks or IPLeaks.

Websites rely on IP databases like Maxmind, IP2Location, IPinfo, etc. to look up your exact location, but these databases get outdated quickly and need constant updating. Most of these databases also correct errors on request, but that takes some time. Also, each website has its own rules for how often it queries IP databases for updates.

Even when locations are updated in the actual database, a website may be sitting with outdated information unless it actively has refreshed it recently. MaxMind recommends doing this at least twice a week, but that’s ultimately up to the specific website.

VPN providers often rely on virtual servers that use IP addresses registered in a different region than the server’s physical location. For example, ExpressVPN notes that it serves IP addresses in Bolivia, Ecuador, Peru, Uruguay, and Venezuela through servers located physically in Brazil.

While this is usually to help with the lack of local infrastructure or strict surveillance laws in the region, many databases and analytics tools will flag the discrepancy for websites, leading to confusion and inconsistency regarding your actual server location. In these cases, your best bet is to switch your VPN server or use a dedicated IP address by signing up for a paid add-on for your VPN.

Before investing in a specific solution, it’s better to do a thorough lookup to make sure why your IP address is pointing to the wrong location and whether a WebRTC or DNS issue, virtual servers, or something else is the cause. Here’s how I’d go about it, step by step:

We’ll start by recording your baseline IP address, country, city, and ASN. With your VPN disconnected, navigate to IPConfig.co on your browser to grab your default IP details. I like this tool because it’s free and uses echoip to pull your location data straight from Maxmind. If you prefer using Terminal instead of your browser, you can also run curl ifconfig.co/json to grab these details in plain text format.

Now connect your VPN server. Then visit IPConfig.co again to note down the changed details.

Make sure the Country and Timezone fields match your current VPN server location. Also make sure the ASN (organization) field does not list your home ISP.

In case these details do not match your VPN server, run a WebRTC leak test and a DNS leak test using BrowserLeaks. If either of these tests doesn’t return your VPN server’s IP address, you’ve found your culprit.

If both the leak tests come back clean but a specific site still gets your location wrong, the issue is likely originating from an outdated database or virtual server.

There’s no surefire way to solve IP location mismatches for good. But if it happens often enough to disrupt your internet use, you can consider switching to a different VPN server and getting a browser add-on that prevents WebRTC leaks. If that doesn’t work, I’d consider buying a dedicated IP address that isn’t based on a virtual server. While many IP databases like MaxMind or IP2Location also have ways to request a location correction, that takes a while to go through and isn’t worth the effort unless it’s a permanent IP address registered through your ISP.

President Trump's Defamation Claim Against BBC Can Go Forward

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

From today's decision by Judge Jeffrey Kuntz (S.D. Fla.) in Trump v. BBC:

In Trump v. CNN (S.D. Fla. 2023), aff'd (11th Cir. 2025), Judge Singhal concluded that "the complained of statements are opinion, not factually false statements, and therefore are not actionable." In Trump v. Dow Jones & Co., Inc. (S.D. Fla. 2026), Judge Gayles dismissed President Trump's claims that the Wall Street Journal failed to adequately investigate claims it reported.

Those cases involved a news entity reporting on a story. Here, the news entity created the story. BBC admitted as much by confirming it had edited a video to make it appear as though President Trump made a statement he never made.

And it acknowledged it chose to release the video in a documentary only a week before the 2024 Presidential Election, at the time it would have the most impact. To make sure it was seen in Florida, BBC even emailed U.S. and Florida based commenters with instructions to on how to view the documentary.

Whether President Trump can prove these claims and establish he sustained damages at summary judgment or trial is left for another day. For now, after careful review, and being otherwise fully advised, BBC's Motion to Dismiss is granted [as to the defamation claim] ….

On October 28, 2024, one week before the 2024 Presidential Election, BBC broadcast a documentary titled "Trump: A Second Chance." In the Documentary, a twelve-second segment portrayed President Trump giving a speech to his supporters on January 6, 2021:

We're going to walk down to the Capitol and I'll be there with you. And we fight. We fight like hell and if you don't fight like hell, you're not going to have a country anymore.

This two sentence, twelve-second segment, represents the core of Trump's complaint. However, the issue with the statement is that Trump never said it. The above statement is actually two parts of a speech spliced together. Instead, the first remarks that Trump made were:

Now it is up to Congress to confront this egregious assault on our democracy. And after this, we're going to walk down and I'll be there with you. We're going to walk down, we're going to walk down. Anyone you want but I think right here, we're going to walk down to the Capitol and we're going to cheer on our brave senators and congressmen and women, and we're probably not going to be cheering so much for some of them.

Nearly fifty-five minutes later, Trump continued:

[B]ut I said 'Something's wrong here, Something's really wrong, can't have happened.' And we fight, we fight like hell, and if you don't fight like hell, you're not going to have a country anymore.

Additionally, less than a minute later, Trump told his supporters to peacefully walk to the Capitol, stating:

I know that everyone here will soon be marching over to the Capitol building to peacefully and patriotically make your voices heard[.]

It is those two statements, edited and spliced together, that form the alleged defamatory twelve-second segment that aired in the Documentary. BBC admits Trump never made the statements as aired. The Chairman of BBC apologized to Trump, stating that the BBC "editing unintentionally created the impression of a single continuous section of your speech, rather than excerpts from different points in the speech." This, the BBC chairman stated, "gave the mistaken impression that you had made a direct call for violent action. That was an editorial error and it should not have happened." …

The court concluded that President Trump had adequately alleged actual damages and "actual malice" (i.e., knowing and reckless falsehood) on BBC's part, though of course the court noted that both would still have to be proved eventually as a factual matter. An excerpt:

For purposes of the defamation test, ill-will is different from actual malice. An intention to portray a public figure in a negative light, even when motivated by ill-will or evil intent, is not sufficient to show actual malice unless the publisher intended to inflict harm through knowing or reckless falsehood. However, ill-will or motive, when combined with other evidence, may be taken into account for actual malice….

Here, Trump alleges that BBC has a history of unfair or improper coverage toward him. In addition to the Documentary itself, Trump highlights the Prescott Memorandum, a 2022 BBC Newsnight episode, and the "2024 BBC News Shooting Hoax." As an important reminder, the state of mind required for actual malice must be "brought home" to the person in the BBC's organization responsible for the publication of the Documentary. This principle is relevant as it relates to the 2022 Newsnight episode and the 2024 "Shooting Hoax." Again, Trump has not alleged that either of these incidents involved the creator(s) of the Documentary. That said, these incidents are not merely portrayal in a negative light, but examples of BBC playing fast and loose with the factual accuracy of their media content. With respect to the Newsnight episode, the BBC seemingly did not care about the falsity of the portrayal until the later Documentary became an issue. Such examples can be said to be probative of BBC's state of mind as it pertains to recklessness in light of BBC's non-action toward false statements about Trump.

Thus, Trump paints a picture that demonstrates the BBC has a record of allowing the publication of false statements in their portrayal of Trump. BBC asks the Court to put on blinders and treat the Panorama team as if they were blocked off from the rest of the organization, ignorant of everything else going on at BBC. At this procedural stage [i.e., a motion to dismiss, where plaintiff must merely plausibly allege facts that could lead to liability, rather than proving them -EV], the Court simply cannot ignore the circumstantial evidence presented. To do so would make it extremely difficult to ever prove a defendant's subjective state of mind in any defamation case….

Trump alleged that BBC's editing of the speech was an "intentional and malicious effort to falsely and deceptively portray President Trump as having called for violence." In support, Trump alleges that it "would have been impossible for BBC's journalists and producers to splice together two distinct parts of the [s]peech from nearly fifty-five minutes apart unless they were acting intentionally." On its face, the editing may independently establish the plausibility of actual malice. BBC tries to frame this editing as ordinary editing done to condense a seventy-minute-long speech into twelve seconds. But the Court is not persuaded.

Editing is often required. However, given the time disparity between the two statements, the distance itself tends toward a plausible inference that BBC knew or should have known that the segment they were creating would not and could not have been a truthful statement. This was not trimming around the edges but creating something different than what was stated in the speech. BBC also had the entire speech available to them for nearly three years by the time of the Documentary.

In addition to the editing itself, BBC's Chairman issued an apology:

The way the speech was edited in the documentary gave a misleading impression of what you said. The editing unintentionally created the impression of a single continuous section of your speech, rather than excerpts from different points in the speech. This gave the mistaken impression that you had made a direct call for violent action. That was an editorial error and it should not have happened.

However, the BBC now appears to disagree with the apology issued by the Chairman. BBC's Chairman admitted that the documentary gave a misleading and mistaken impression that Trump made a direct call for violent action. Strangely enough, BBC strays from that position in the Motion to Dismiss, referring to statements made by January 6 defendants as evidence that the speech may be interpreted as a call to violent action. But BBC's own Chairman acknowledged the editing was misleading in his letter to Trump….

As a final matter on the issue of actual malice, Trump alleges that BBC strayed from accepted standards of journalistic integrity. Such a claim on its own would not amount to actual malice, since actual malice requires "more than a departure from reasonable journalistic standards." A failure to investigate, standing on its own, does not indicate the presence of actual malice. To show actual malice, there must be some showing that the BBC purposefully avoided further investigation with the intent to avoid the truth.

BBC first argues that allegations BBC failed to contact Trump for comment before publishing are insufficient to establish actual malice. On this point, the Court agrees.

BBC then argues that allegations that BBC omitted portions of Trump's speech are also insufficient to establish actual malice. Alterations of quotes that "materially alter" the meaning of Trump's statements could support a finding of actual malice…. BBC did not just alter the quote but created a new quote by splicing two statements together and passing them off as a single continuous sentence. To the extent that there was no material alteration to the meaning of Trump's speech, that simply cannot be the case. The Chairman's letter makes it very clear that BBC acknowledges there was a material change of meaning when they edited the speech to create a single continuous segment that did not exist.

BBC then argues that the apology "militates against a finding of actual malice." … Today, retractions can be issued near instantaneously for online publications thanks to the internet. The Court acknowledges that media entities, like The Washington Post, or BBC, are not perfect, and make mistakes. However, it cannot be said that a retraction the next day is in the same ballpark as a retraction over a year later, and only after being threatened with legal action. The temporal distance here makes the retraction a weak indicator as to BBC's state of mind at the time of publication.

Further weakening BBC's argument, BBC was aware that such editing created a false impression as early as 2022. Former Acting White House Chief of Staff Mick Mulvaney, in relation to the 2022 Newsnight episode, stated on-air: "Your video actually spliced together the presentation.… [Y]our video makes it look like those two things came together." As BBC's own Clive Coleman stated "[t]he first lesson … you're taught as a broadcast journalist is that you do not joint two bits of footage together from different times in a way that will make the audience think that it is one piece of footage." Considering these facts, BBC's ultimate retraction in 2025 reads less like a readiness to retract, and more like a reluctance to retract.

The Court acknowledges that events from 2022 do not directly bear on the state of mind at the time of publication. But neither does a retraction over a year after publication. Hence why courts are often reliant on circumstantial evidence when making state of mind determinations. Still, Trump's allegations regarding BBC's departure from journalistic standards are more than enough to plausibly alleged more than mere negligence….

Of course, all of this does not prove BBC acted with actual malice. But it does make it plausible. And Trump need only allege facts sufficient to give rise to a reasonable inference that the BBC edited and published the Documentary "with knowledge that it was false or with reckless disregard of whether it was false or not." Trump has done so here….

And the court held the BBC's statement could be viewed as a false factual allegation, rather than merely "convey[ing] an opinion of what the audience understood Trump to say":

As Trump correctly points out, a spliced segment that creates a sentence that Trump never spoke represents the exact kind of false statement of fact that courts hold as the sine qua non for recovery in defamation. Whether the audience "reasonably interpreted" the segment to convey that Trump called for violence is irrelevant….

The court also held that Florida courts (including the federal courts) had jurisdiction over the BBC. But the court rejected Trump's Florida Deceptive and Unfair Trade Practices Act claim:

… FDUTPA is limited to acts "in the conduct of any trade or commerce." The statute defines this as "the advertising, soliciting, providing, offering, or distributing" of goods, services, property, "or any other article, commodity, or thing of value." … "[M]agazines and newspapers often have commercial purposes, but those purposes do not convert the individual articles within these editorial sources into commercial speech." Both advertising and subscriptions are typical features of newspapers, whether online or in-print. Even if BBC has commercial purposes, that does not convert the Documentary into commercial speech….

Alejandro Brito, Ian Michael Corp, Jalaine Garcia, and Michael O. Mena (Brito, PLLC), Daniel Epstein (Epstein & Co.), Edward Andrew Paltzik (Tylor Dykema) represent Trump.

Schwartz Distinguished Professor of Law Emeritus and Distinguished Research Professor at UCLA School of Law. CoreWeave arrests stock slide after posting a strong earnings beat and raising its full-year guidance

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Neocloud infrastructure darling CoreWeave Inc. delivered an impressive revenue beat and raised its full-year guidance today as it demonstrated that demand for artificial intelligence computing remains strong, pushing its stock higher in late trading.

The report comes at a prickly moment for the AI industry as investors increasingly sour on heavily indebted cloud infrastructure startups, while the broader market continues to get to grips with the spy scandal surrounding Nvidia Corp. Chief Executive Jensen Huang.

The company reported a third-quarter loss before certain costs such as stock compensation of $1.27 per share, trailing Wall Street’s target of a $1.16 per share loss. But the 168% growth in revenue meant sales came to $3.66 billion, topping expectations of $3.55 billion. All told, CoreWeave reported a net loss in the quarter of $709 million, rising from a $110 million net loss in the same period one year earlier.

Investors may be pleased with CoreWeave’s guidance, though. Company executives said they’re now looking for between $1.275 billion and $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income and $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue for the full year. Wall Street analysts are looking for just $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income and $12.86 billion in total sales. CoreWeave’s forecast is notably much higher than the $960 million to $1.15 billion in adjusted operating income and $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion in revenue it had forecast three months earlier.

The report was well-received by investors, as CoreWeave’s stock climbed more than 5% in late trading, helping put the brakes on a slide that has seen the company lose more than 40% of its value over the last month. Many investors have sought to abandon stocks that are closely tied to the AI infrastructure buildout amid fears that rising interest rates have made capital much more difficult to come by. Recently, some CoreWeave bond yields have topped 16%.

CoreWeave Chief Executive Michael Intrator (pictured) told analysts on a conference call that most of the company’s capital requirements in fiscal 2027 will be sourced from clients via pre-payments on infrastructure services and from hardware partners such as Nvidia. He added that the company will also seek to raise a “limited amount” in outside funding.

When pressed on this, CoreWeave’s finance chief Nitin Agrawal said the company plans to raise new debt that will be tied to a set of contracts. “At current yields we do not intend to issue additional parent-level unsecured debt through 2027,” he told analysts. “By contrast, the asset-level secured debt market has remained stable, with our loans continuing to trade around par value.”

CoreWeave, along with other neoclouds like Nebius Group N.V., which is set to report its own earnings results on Thursday, has become increasingly reliant on its hardware partner Nvidia as a major benefactor. Nvidia provides CoreWeave with not only the graphics processing units needed to populate its data centers, but also much of the capital it needs to procure those chips.

Eessentially it’s recycling the money it earns throughout the AI economy in order to keep everything ticking over. But investors have become concerned about the long-term viability of what looks like an increasingly circular economy, and Nvidia’s stock has struggled to maintain momentum this year.

With this week’s revelations that Nvidia boss Jensen Huang had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, the chipmaker’s stock has lost almost 10% of its value to start the week. Nvidia’s board of directors has responded by launching an internal investigation, hiring the services of the high-profile legal firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to look into Huang’s personal life and determine if any company or national security secrets were compromised.

The fallout from that probe will likely have a strong bearing on CoreWeave’s fortunes going forward, as will next week’s widely anticipated initial public offering from AI leader Anthropic PBC. In an update to its IPO prospectus earlier today, the model maker said it’s hoping to achieve a $1.4 trillion valuation through its share sale.

In the meantime, CoreWeave continues to forge ahead with its buildout. During the quarter it said it’s planning to build its first data centers in Asia. It also announced a new cloud-based service called Forge for running and evaluating AI models.

The company said it hopes to have 1.85 gigawatts of active compute power in service by the end of the year, and anticipates its capital expenditure will reach between $37 billion and $39 billion. That’s more or less in line with the Street’s forecast of $37.69 billion in capex this year.

At the end of the quarter, CoreWeave’s total debt was approaching $47 billion. Like its much larger peers in the cloud computing industry, such as Amazon Web Services Inc., Microsoft Corp. and Oracle Corp., CoreWeave is racing to add more compute capacity to serve its clients.

But there are signs it may struggle to do this in the U.S. Last week, American voters either backed restrictions on data center construction or rejected pro-data center development measures in 40 of 41 states.

Intrator addressed those concerns in the conference call, insisting that he wasn’t overly concerned. “If data center construction across the sector were to slow, installed compute becomes scarcer and the knock-on effect is that GPU pricing may stay higher for longer,” he said.

In the meantime, CoreWeave continues to see demand for cloud compute escalate, and is now sitting on a massive $132.5 billion revenue backlog, Intrator said. That’s up from just $104 billion three months earlier.

PlayStation adds live-service shooter Fairgames to its constantly expanding graveyard

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sony's trail of bodies is only getting longer

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Sony has dug up another plot in its expanding live-service graveyard, and is lowering the still-warm corpse of Haven Studios' Fairgames into the dirt. The shooter, which was revealed in 2023, has been canceled, though PlayStation says there will be no layoffs as a result of its decision to end the project.

"The studio will shift its focus to collaborating with other development teams across PlayStation Studios, while also exploring new creative opportunities," a PlayStation representative tells Bloomberg, who first reported the news.

Fairgames was revealed in a 2023 trailer which stylized the title as "Fairgame$." The trailer featured no in-game footage, but presented a stylish version of a Payday-style shooter focused on heists. But the game never materialized again. The last major news on the project came in 2025, when Haven Studios founder Jade Raymond – a veteran Ubisoft producer who'd worked on Assassins' Creed – left the company.

Bloomberg reports that Fairgames was ultimately canceled after a poor showing during a recent test session with thousands of players. "While the test demonstrated strong operational excellence, live ops readiness and community engagement, player feedback and retention results were mixed and fell short of the goals we had set," according to an internal memo.

PlayStation had apparently reworked the game multiple times since it was first revealed, and prior to the test had planned to showcase it again under a new title, according to the report.

While PlayStation once had extensive plans for live-service games, it's canceled so many of those projects that it's now difficult to keep track of them all. The death of Concord just two weeks after launch is perhaps the highest profile example, but that's just the most memorable corpse among an extensive trail of bodies.

A multiplayer God of War title from Bluepoint and a live-service game from Bend Studio were canceled in 2025. A multitude of unannounced projects were iced amid mass layoffs at PlayStation in 2024, including a Twisted Metal game and an original project from the publisher's now-defunct London studio. Naughty Dog's The Last of Us multiplayer game died in 2023. Bungie has reportedly had numerous projects scrapped since it was acquired by Sony. PlayStation has even killed not just one, but two projects helmed by Call of Duty veteran Jason Blundell.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

Sony's biggest live-service hit in recent years is certainly Helldivers 2, but it's struggled to replicate that success – heck, it's struggled to release other online games at any scale. The publisher's annual MLB The Show series remains a staple among baseball fans, Marvel Tokon: Fighting Souls seems to have found a niche among fighting game aficionados, and Marathon is continuing to survive, but these aren't the Fortnite-level hits Sony is clearly gunning for. I guess we'll never know whether Fairgames could've been a hit.

Why AAON Stock Popped Today

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Effective heat control systems are an increasingly vital component of the AI boom.

Shares of AAON (AAON +19.31%) leaped 19% on Tuesday after the heating, ventilation, and air conditioning (HVAC) equipment maker boosted its full-year growth forecast.

AAON's net sales soared 84% year over year to $707 million in the third quarter.

Growth in the company's artificial intelligence (AI)-focused BASX segment was particularly robust, with net sales up a whopping 207% to $231 million.

AAON's high-performance thermal management solutions help to keep data centers cool. The HVAC infrastructure supplier has been ramping up its manufacturing capacity to meet the surging demand for its products.

"Compute density is increasing, power availability remains constrained, efficiency matters more, and thermal management becomes increasingly more important as these systems become more power dense," CEO Matt Tobolski said during a conference call with analysts.

AAON's impressive sales growth, combined with its cost-containment initiatives, drove its profit margins higher. Operating income rocketed 129% to $100 million, while adjusted earnings per share climbed 124% to $0.83.

These strong results prompted AAON to lift its full-year guidance. Management now sees net sales growing by 72% to 75% in 2026, up from a prior forecast of 55% to 60%.

"Customer engagement remains strong, our opportunity pipeline is healthy, and we continue to strengthen our position with strategic data center customers," CEO Matt Tobolski said.

Is ‘I Play Rocky’ Original or Adapted? Stallone’s ‘Rocky’ Origin Story Faces a ‘Barbie’-Style Oscar Dilemma (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sylvester Stallone wrote “Rocky” in three days. Figuring out which Oscar screenplay category the movie about him writing it belongs in is taking a lot longer.

Amazon MGM Studios’ “I Play Rocky” has been the subject of multiple conversations among the studio, the Writers Guild of America, the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences and the film’s creative team over whether Peter Gamble’s script will compete as an original or adapted screenplay. No determination has been made. Related Stories Trump Says 'Artificial Intelligence' Is 'a Very Bad and Inaccurate Name,' and That He Wants to Officially Ban the Term 'AI' 'Harry Potter' HBO Series Casts Sorting Hat, Nearly Headless Nick, and More Hogwarts Ghosts and Portraits

Thursday, Nov. 12, is the final deadline for studios to submit Official Screen Credits (OSC) forms to the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences for the 99th Academy Awards in the best picture, animated feature and general entry categories. No films afterward will be considered.

Gamble conceived and wrote the script as an original, and it isn’t based on a book, article, memoir or any other single published work. He drafted it on spec, and a spot on the Black List carried it to producers Christian Baha and Toby Emmerich and eventually to director Peter Farrelly, who helmed best picture winner “Green Book” (2018). It’s Gamble’s second produced feature script after “Office Uprising” (2018).

But “Rocky” is one of the most established pieces of IP in Hollywood, and sources say some within each organization are weighing whether the screenplay is a better fit for adapted, the category for scripts built on previously established material. Rocky Balboa, Adrian and Apollo Creed are preexisting characters and Farrelly’s film restages some of the 1976 movie’s best-known scenes, written half a century ago.

Amazon MGM and the Academy declined to comment.

“I Play Rocky” expands nationwide Nov. 20, the same day WGA submissions close, but the guild won’t be weighing in. The film wasn’t produced under a WGA signatory agreement, so the guild can’t classify the screenplay or consider it for the WGA Awards, even though Gamble is a member. That leaves the decision entirely to the Academy’s Writers Branch executive committee, which makes its own determinations and has broken with the WGA before.

Elsewhere on the circuit, the question will carry less weight. The Golden Globes put all screenplays in a single category, while BAFTA and the Critics Choice Awards, which split original and adapted, generally go with the category the studio submits.

According to the Academy’s screenplay submission FAQ for the 99th Oscars, films don’t submit to a specific writing category. Instead, the Writers Branch executive committee reviews each eligible title and generally defers to the WGA’s classification, except when the guild hasn’t classified a film, as is the case with “I Play Rocky” or its classification “has been called into question.” The guidelines cut both ways for Gamble’s script.

A script is generally deemed adapted if it’s based on a sequel, a remake, an existing film or “pre-existing characters,” but it’s considered original if it tells a true story in which the writer “performed their own research and used multiple sources.” While a determination is under review, the committee may ask the film’s credited writers for a statement. Once it rules, the decision is final and no appeals or additional materials are accepted.

We seem to have one of these every awards season.

In January 2024, Greta Gerwig’s “Barbie” was moved to adapted after Warner Bros. campaigned it as an original and the WGA classified as well. Barbie and Ken existed as Mattel dolls long before Gerwig and Noah Baumbach turned them into a billion-dollar sensation, and there were multiple cartoons that existed with the characters. Ultimately, the script earned an adapted nomination and lost to Cord Jefferson’s “American Fiction.”

Category reversals aren’t new. Barry Jenkins’ “Moonlight,” Damien Chazelle’s “Whiplash” and Joel and Ethan Coen’s “The Ballad of Buster Scruggs” all carried original labels from the WGA before the Academy shifted them to adapted. The Academy has also long treated sequels as adaptations because their characters come from earlier films, which is how “Before Sunset,” “Toy Story 3,” “Top Gun: Maverick” and “Glass Onion” all landed in adapted.

That rule is being tested again this season. Aaron Sorkin’s “The Social Reckoning,” which dramatizes whistleblower Frances Haugen’s 2021 Facebook leak and Wall Street Journal reporter Jeff Horwitz’s coverage of it, was initially marketed as a sequel to “The Social Network” (2010), which won Sorkin the adapted screenplay Oscar. The WGA has classified the new film as an original. There’s no word yet on whether the Academy’s committee will accept that designation.

That’s the precedent Gamble’s script has to get around, and there’s a strong counterargument. “I Play Rocky” isn’t a sequel, a prequel or a spinoff. It’s a biographical drama about a real person, and biopics have routinely competed as originals. Dustin Lance Black won original screenplay for “Milk,” even though Harvey Milk’s life had already been chronicled in the Oscar-winning documentary “The Times of Harvey Milk.” Anthony McCarten’s “Darkest Hour” also competed as an original, despite covering Winston Churchill’s first weeks as prime minister, a chapter that fills every high school history textbook.

Movies about the making of movies are interesting territory, and they have produced originals, too. “Saving Mr. Banks,” about the making of “Mary Poppins,” competed as an original but didn’t land a nomination. Neither did Jason Reitman and Gil Kenan’s “Saturday Night,” which follows the 90 minutes leading up to the first televised episode of “Saturday Night Live.” Netflix’s “Mank,” written by the late Jack Fincher, father of director David Fincher, chronicles screenwriter Herman J. Mankiewicz’s work on Orson Welles’ “Citizen Kane” (1941). It was also deemed an original, and it also missed in the category, even as the film led the field with 10 nominations.

When a making-of film does land in adapted, there’s usually a book behind it. Scott Neustadter and Michael H. Weber’s “The Disaster Artist,” for example, was based on Greg Sestero and Tom Bissell’s 2013 nonfiction book of the same name. A public figure’s life, however, is fair game, as illustrated by A24’s Chris Hansen story, “Primetime,” which Ajon Singh based on an article by Luke Dittrich.

Interestingly, Amazon MGM owns the “Rocky” franchise. The 1976 original earned 10 Oscar nominations and won best picture, and Stallone was nominated for best actor and original screenplay. Director Farrelly knows the original side all too well, as he shared the original screenplay Oscar with Brian Hayes Currie and Nick Vallelonga for “Green Book,” another true story that drew from no single published source.

Where the film lands could reshape its trajectory, and both races are stacked. In original, Jesse Eisenberg (“The Debut”), Jordan Firstman (“Club Kid”), Mike Leigh (“Tender Loving Care”), Martin McDonagh (“Wild Horse Nine”) and Simon Rich (“Artificial”) are all in the conversation. A move to adapted wouldn’t clear the road. It would put Gamble up against Christopher Nolan’s “The Odyssey,” Drew Goddard’s “Project Hail Mary” and Rashida Jones and Will McCormack’s “The Invite,” along with Los Javis’ “La Bola Negra” and Denis Villeneuve’s yet-to-be-screened “Dune: Part Three.”

“I Play Rocky” has been gaining momentum since its world premiere at TIFF. It finished first runner-up for the People’s Choice Award behind “La Bola Negra” and was the runner-up at Middleburg. It was also the audience choice winner at Cinéfest Sudbury International Film Festival, and recently Ippolito was honored with the breakthrough award at SCAD Savannah Film Festival. It recently opened on Nov. 6 in seven theaters, capturing $345,000 from seven theaters, averaging $49,200 per location. The film has also made one category call already: it’s submitted at the Golden Globes as a musical or comedy, joining a field that includes “Wild Horse Nine,” “The Debut” and Amazon MGM stablemate “Project Hail Mary.”

The Academy’s final word likely won’t surface until nominations voting opens Jan. 11, 2027. Before that, the WGA announces its screenplay nominees Jan. 8. Oscar nominations will be announced Jan. 21, and the 99th Oscars take place March 14.

Romano: "Inzaghi-Napoli e Juve? Ho chiesto a Simone su addio all'Al Hilal e mi è stato detto..."

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Romano: "Inzaghi-Napoli e Juve? Ho chiesto a Simone su addio all'Al Hilal e mi è stato detto..."

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

Sul suo canale YouTube, Fabrizio Romano ha fatto il punto sulle insistenti voci sul futuro di due allenatori italiani (ex Inter) molto chiacchierati in queste settimane, come Antonio Conte e Simone Inzaghi. Ecco le sue parole:

"Conte-United? La situazione di Carrick può cambiare da un momento all'altro. L'esonero è nell'aria, manca ancora un passaggio formale per ratificare la decisione, ma la strada sembra chiara. Per Emery lo United avrebbe fatto follie, ma lui è contento di restare. Al momento posso dire che Conte non è in trattativa avanzata con lo United, vedremo se le cose cambieranno. Non mi aspetto una scelta legata ai soldi da parte di Conte: aveva a settembre sul tavolo un'offerta vicina ai 20 milioni di euro annui dall'Al Ahly, ma vuole un progetto in un top club. Juventus? In queste settimane o mesi non mi risulta che i bianconeri abbiano parlato con Conte. E' normale accostare la Juventus a Conte, ma non c'è oggi una trattativa. E' una suggestione che resta: se Spalletti non dovesse riuscire a risollevare la situazione, la Juventus potrebbe andare a cercare un allenatore top e chissà che Conte non sia nella lista".

"Inzaghi? E' circolata la voce di un addio a fine stagione all'Al Hilal e ho interpellato i diretti interessati. Dal punto di vista dell'allenatore, oggi non c'è alcuna decisione definitiva, e la smentita arriva direttamente da Inzaghi e da chi gli sta vicino. Può succedere che andrà via e a me non stupirebbe, anche per una questione ambientale, per ciò che sta succedendo in Medio Oriente. Inzaghi oggi non ha deciso nulla, ma credo che sia concreta la possibilità che lui vada via a fine stagione. Se dovesse lasciare l'Arabia, penserebbe all'Italia, anche perché è stato lì due anni e non è facile, anche per questioni familiari. Attenzione comunque a come finirà la stagione, visto che, in caso di stagione molto positiva, il club potrebbe proporre un rinnovo a cifre importanti allo stesso Inzaghi. Ma, come detto, resta la possibilità che lui vada via a fine stagione. Napoli? Inzaghi non è mai stato un nome fin qui in corsa, ma che possa piacere non lo escludo affatto, così come che possa piacere alla Juventus. Ma oggi sono discorsi anticipati rispetto alla realtà".

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Big Walk AI Controversy Is a GOTY Awards Twist Sparking a Bigger Debate

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An IGF voter stance marks a new hardline for the industry

Big Walk, one of the best-reviewed games of 2026 and by all measures a Game of the Year darling, faces a crossroads on the eve of awards season. Following a recent disclosure of AI use from its developer, Big Walk’s chances at one awards show seem fraught. And its shot at the big one (December’s Game Awards) might be impacted. But it’s not just a chapter in 2026’s awards show saga. In many ways, it’s a boiling over of a debate that’s been bubbling in the games industry for several years now.

On Monday, a group of 19 Independent Game Festival (IGF) jurors, including Immortality director Sam Barlow and Friends at the Table host Austin Walker, shared a statement saying they would not vote for any game that used generative AI tools in any capacity — including for code.

On Bluesky, Barlow said that this discussion among IGF jurors is always lively, but that this year’s “conversation was even more intense.”

Why? It did not take long for at least part of the reason to become clear: Big Walk used generative AI tools in development, developer House House revealed in its voluntary disclosure to the IGF. (In a follow-up statement, Barlow noted that the impetus for the conversation was more broadly about the volume of games that make use of generative AI, and that Big Walk was the "tricky edge case.")

In a statement to Aftermath (also cross-posted on the developer’s website), House House said that “well under 0.1% of Big Walk’s code” had anything to do with generative AI tools, and those tools were used solely to “help work through difficult technical challenges and bugs.” House House also noted that the studio used large language models (LLMs) to generate a baseline, which the team then “modified into game code.” House House’s statement gets into it in full, but the studio pointed to a lighting issue as an example of how LLMs helped with the final phase of Big Walk’s development, amounting to “about 10 lines of code.”

That might seem small in the grand scheme of things, but a hard line is only a hard line if you don’t cross it. And for 19 members of the IGF jury, that’s the line.

The group of jurors cite several reasons for its stance against generative AI, including the technology’s widely documented negative impacts on the economy, the labor market, the environment, and the global informational pipeline. They also say that AI use is “not a foregone conclusion” in the games industry, and, as independent developers, see this as an opportunity to take a stance that many people working at larger companies might not have the leeway to take.

Critically, the inclusion of code as part of the hardline is not something you see every day. Generally, the colloquial argument against generative AI use is reserved for disciplines that are considered more “creative” in nature: art, writing, composition, voice acting, and so forth. You often see people make exceptions for technical work, like programming. This stance from part of the IGF jury marks a clear public expansion of the tent among an influential games industry group, and it’s not lost among a subset of the community that isn’t always public-facing. Responses to Walker’s Bluesky post of the statement are full of programmers thanking the jurors for considering coding an art form.

The debate isn’t merely broken down among clean lines of “AI good” vs. “AI bad.” Some see it as a betrayal not just that House House used these tools but that it didn’t disclose that use upon release. If the use of an LLM was no big deal, the argument goes, why hide it? And if the company hid this instance, what else was hidden? (Though Steam requires a disclosure for generative AI use, it does not require that disclosure for the use of code or chatbots.)

For others, it’s just about the best case scenario for a worst-case situation. It’s a fairly minimal use, they say, and if strict delineations are made for the public disclosure of 10 lines of AI-generated code, wouldn’t that disqualify many video games? Plus, they say, House House seems reflective and open to change, pointing to the studio’s philanthropic efforts in the past as a sign of good will. (In 2020, following the release of Untitled Goose Game, House House pledged to donate 1% of its profits to organizations that help members of indigenous communities make rent.)

Will this ultimately affect Big Walk’s chances during awards season? Maybe! Though the statement was issued by fewer than 20 IGF jury members, it’s gained traction across both social and traditional media. And now that it’s become a matter of public interest and widespread discussion, there’s always the chance other voters are swayed by their arguments or otherwise hesitate to vote for Big Walk.

How this affects the Game Awards horse race is a cloudier issue. On Monday, Big Walk was all but a lock for a Game of the Year nomination, according to Polygon’s ongoing analysis of the race. But an indie game securing a GOTY nomination is always an uphill race — even without the albatross of controversy.

Yes, the technically indie game Clair Obscur: Expedition 33 smashed records and swept the 2025 Game Awards, but it also broke into the mainstream in an undeniable way. Big Walk, despite its impressive critical reception, has not. To secure the nomination, it needs every vote it can get from nominating jurors who are part of the indie gaming community, which largely rejects generative AI. To wit: Clair Obscur may have run the tale during the 2025 awards circuit, but ultimately had its Game of the Year award from the Indie Game Awards (note: not the same thing as the IGF) stripped over inclusion of AI.

Beyond awards, the entire saga seems an inflection point for an industry that has struggled to grapple with the encroachment of generative AI. Developers of all strata — from the creator of The Last of Us to the studio behind the Wendy’s JRPG — have taken public stances against the technology, refusing to use AI whatsoever in any part of development. They stand in contrast to prominent firms like Embark Studios, which caught heat for using AI in its 2023 shooter The Finals and then immediately used the same tools for its 2025 shooter Arc Raiders, or Epic Games, which is run by a CEO whose stance on LLMs could fairly be summarized as “Claude = Jesus 2.0.”

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

All the while, AI tools have proven a third rail for large portions of the gaming audience, often prompting public apologies from developers that disclose their use. House House joins that club.

“Big Walk was developed without an internal AI usage policy in place, and we consider that lack of policy a failure,” House House said in its statement. “We will work to have a more comprehensive policy going forwards.”

That’s good on House House. Stating some clearly defined rules for this sort of thing helps settle internal debate, and provides a north star for anyone working at the studio. It could even serve as a model for others in the industry to adopt. But the lack of policy shouldn’t land entirely on the shoulders of a small outfit in Melbourne — or any developer of any size, for that matter. At this point, generative AI tools have been publicly available for nearly half a decade. Until governments start taking the technology seriously and passing real regulations around what counts as acceptable use, this will keep happening.

Bruce Straley isn't all too fond of generative AI art

Why the Adobe MAX 2026 opening keynote has me feeling cautiously hopeful.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Adobe MAX is in full swing, and after this morning's keynote, I'm left feeling pleasantly surprised. I initially arrived with slight apprehension, expecting to leave fatigued and underwhelmed by the grand kickoff, especially regarding Adobe's ever-divisive AI developments. Instead, I must confess, I'm cautiously hopeful.

It's no secret Adobe is going full send on AI, after a full fleet of AI partnerships and Firefly taking centre stage, blossoming into a full-fledged creative studio. It could all start to feel a little existential, but beaming under the stage lights, future Adobe CEO Anil Chakravarthy assures me, "Adobe will meet you where you are”. And I start to believe him.

AI has persisted as a divisive topic among creatives, but Adobe has shown a refreshing self-awareness around the tension. Emerging on stage, vice president Deepa Subramaniam wastes no time addressing that "things feel uncertain” in the wake of AI.

Assurances of “reliability” and “safety” are thrown around, honing in on Adobe's legacy as a brand shaped and "built by creatives that care about the craft”. She assures the sceptics among the crowd (myself included) that for Adobe, "Creativity isn’t a side project; it’s a way of life,” and to my surprise, the typically eye-roll-worthy sentiment soothes me. At its core, it seems Adobe hasn't forgotten about the creatives that shaped its brand.

Deepa raises some valid points. In an increasingly content-driven world, more is asked of us. The humble graphic designer is now expected to be a web designer, motion designer, and social media maverick all in one – something that Adobe's “rich learning ecosystem” can solve, according to Deepa. Creatives are trapped in a battle with waning time and resources, with demand for content ever increasing. If handled correctly, time-saving AI could offer the respite we need, but it raises a moral dilemma. Where do we stop?

For more Adobe news, check out why Adobe's Google partnership hints at a radically different future for Photoshop or take a look at the new AI features coming to Photoshop.

‘It’s Like Facebook Again’: How AI IPOs Could Further Upend the Real Estate Market

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A robotic hand presents a house icon with a percentage symbol, symbolizing growth and investment in a modern office space, merging technology with real estate. Synapse

(TNS)– The deluge of wealth the artificial intelligence boom has brought into San Francisco has already pushed luxury home prices to record heights. Now, with Anthropic’s initial public offering poised to bring even more money into the city, could things get even worse?

Real estate professionals say yes—but that a lot of the growth is already baked in.

Let’s take stock of what’s already happened. San Francisco’s home prices have risen most sharply in neighborhoods that were already exclusive, such as Pacific Heights or the Marina. The median home price for luxury homes in the San Francisco metropolitan area—properties sold in San Francisco and San Mateo counties at or above the 95th percentile price—was nearly $7.5 million from July to September 2026, Redfin data shows. That was up a whopping 23% from the same period in 2025, and up more than 60% from 2019.

Now, some experts suggest that AI IPOs could cause demand to spill even further, raising prices for the few home types—or communities—in the Bay Area where middle-income families might still afford a property.

Already, some data indicates that even non-luxury homes in the city have seen strong appreciation over the past year.

“Typical” San Francisco area homes—those in the 35th-to-65th percentile sale price range — saw a record median sale price of $1.6 million in September 2026, an increase of 6.5% from 2025. While that’s a much lower jump compared to the San Francisco area’s luxury market, it still diverges sharply from that of the rest of the United States, where high mortgage rates and economic woes have caused prices to dip.

Redfin economist Daryl Fairweather believes the heat in San Francisco will spread to other parts of the region. In some ways, it already has; the city’s surging rents have caused many tenants to seek refuge in relatively more affordable cities like Oakland, pushing up prices there.

“This has been the story for at least … 15 years: San Francisco prices and rents going up and people being displaced,” Fairweather said.

Demand for fancy homes will likely keep surging

Some real estate agents say that the AI workers and investors fighting bidding wars in San Francisco often don’t want to give up on their dream house, even as prices rise. That’s part of the reason demand has flowed into other expensive cities in Marin County or the Peninsula, where there are more fancy homes for the buying. Phil Chen, a real estate broker at Christie’s International Real Estate Sereno, said that demand for Hillsborough has soared, with buyers citing a desire for strong schools — and security.

“It’s my first time hearing, ‘Is the glass bulletproof and are there panic rooms?’” Chen said.

Zillow data indicates that home values in Hillsborough, Menlo Park and Piedmont have risen faster over the past year than any other Bay Area city with at least 10,000 people except San Francisco, suggesting that some wealthy buyers are expanding their search radius. In some cities, such as in Piedmont, starter home values—those in the 5th to 35th percentile—have actually climbed more than top-tier values. Still, a “starter home” in Piedmont still costs more than a mid-tier property in San Francisco.

It’s unclear how much demand could rise for homes that aren’t luxury properties or in expensive neighborhoods. One 2019 study found that IPOs increase demand for high-priced homes near the company’s headquarters, but not for low-priced properties. The study’s results also indicated IPOs increased the probability low-income residents moved to lower-income neighborhoods.

But Fairweather said that junior tech workers with smaller windfalls than their senior peers could start to make offers in parts of the Bay Area where their money will go further. Vanessa Hatvany Kitchen, a real estate agent with Compass’ Team Hatvany Kitchen, said her team has seen more people buying condos and smaller single-family homes in San Francisco, fearing they won’t be able to get a foothold after companies like Anthropic go public.

But much of the demand increase is baked in

If there’s any silver lining for buyers, though, it’s that a lot of those companies’ impact on the Bay Area real estate market is already here. Arrian Binnings, a San Francisco real estate agent at Christie’s International Real Estate Sereno, said many AI workers have been able to get cash by borrowing against their stocks or selling their shares via company-approved tender offers, while investors have benefitted from the strength of the public equity market. That’s consistent with some research showing that merely filing for IPOs can raise home prices near a company’s headquarters.

“I don’t see the IPO as the starting gun of a new race. I see it as … we’re probably already at the 50-yard line,” Binnings said. “But I do still see plenty of room to run.”

IPOs don’t always result in an explosion of wealth. In the months after Uber went public in 2019, the company’s stock price plummeted, erasing billions of dollars in value. A similar fate for a public Anthropic or OpenAI would send a chill through the real estate market, some real estate agents say.

But many buyers are optimistic. Chen said some of his clients plan to sell their newly purchased homes in five years, expecting to increase their budgets from $10 million to $25 million. after the IPO. Those buyers, he added, are relatively junior-level employees.

“It’s like Facebook again, but 10x,” Chen said.

UMD Ranked No. 4 in U.S. for Entrepreneurship

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Annual list also places UMD No. 1 in the Mid-Atlantic and No. 3 among public institutions

COLLEGE PARK, Md., Nov. 10, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- The University of Maryland has risen to an all-time-high of No. 4 in the nation for undergraduate entrepreneurship education, according to the 2027 rankings released Tuesday by The Princeton Review and Entrepreneur magazine.

UMD also placed No. 3 among public universities and has the No. 1 undergraduate entrepreneurship program in the mid-Atlantic and Big Ten. In addition, the university ranks No. 15 overall among graduate entrepreneurship programs and No. 2 in the mid-Atlantic.

This marks the second time UMD has been named in the top 5 and 12th consecutive year in the top 10 of the Top Schools for Undergraduate Entrepreneurship Studies.

"The university's continued recognition reflects the strength and breadth of our campuswide innovation and entrepreneurship (I&E) ecosystem, which continues to adapt each year to best match students' evolving needs and interests," said Dean Chang, chief innovation officer. "Our students have abundant and easily accessible programs, mentors, resources, tools, and funding to meet them wherever they are with whatever they need. From the arts and humanities to engineering, business, science and social innovation, entrepreneurship at UMD is more than just starting companies and not confined to one school or discipline â it is woven into every part of the student experience across our university."

The Princeton Review's methodology considers a range of factors, including academic offerings, experiential learning opportunities, mentorship, competitions, scholarships, faculty credentials and alumni entrepreneurship ventures.

One distinctive feature of UMD's ecosystem is its signature living-learning programs, many focused on innovation and entrepreneurship. In 1999, UMD launched the nation's first living-learning entrepreneurship program, now replicated at more than 20 universities. For two decades, the Terrapin Innovation Leadership Program has provided full scholarships to community college transfer students â primarily first-generation students, many from low-income and immigrant households â seeking to become entrepreneurial leaders. In 2025, the Business, Society & Entrepreneurship program (BSE) in College Park Scholars was relaunched and rebranded, becoming one of the largest living-learning programs on campus. Together, UMD's innovation- and entrepreneurship-focused programs serve more than 2,000 students and create opportunities for formal and informal teams to pursue experiential projects, activities and startup ventures.

"UMD students are exposed early to our university's extensive network of dedicated mentors, unique programs and diverse resources. We can channel their creativity and curiosity to discover opportunities where others see obstacles, encourage careers that seem out of reach and develop unique individuals that are better equipped to excel in a fast moving and increasingly complex world," said Oliver Schlake, director of BSE Scholars and clinical professor of entrepreneurship at the Robert H. Smith School of Business.

Nearly one in four undergraduates â 7,025 students across 92 majors â enrolled in undergraduate entrepreneurship courses at UMD in 2025-26. Those courses were taught by 63 faculty members from 25 departments. Two introductory entrepreneurship courses together enroll more than 4,000 students each year. Offered online or in hybrid formats, the courses enable students to explore entrepreneurship while completing general education requirements and provide a pathway to the minor in technology entrepreneurship, which enrolls approximately 500 students from 10 of UMD's 12 undergraduate colleges.

Beyond traditional entrepreneurship, UMD also offers over 100 courses in innovation-related areas like creativity, entrepreneurial mindset, social value creation, business models and design thinking.

"At UMD, we see entrepreneurship as a mindset that encourages students to think creatively, approach challenges from new perspectives and develop the skills to solve complex problems and take on grand challenges," said Jennifer King Rice, UMD's senior vice president and provost. "We are committed to providing students across disciplines with opportunities to cultivate this mindset through innovative coursework and hands-on learning experiences. This recognition speaks to the strength of our academic programs and the dedication of our faculty to preparing students to turn ideas into meaningful impact."

Other key components of UMD's award-winning I&E ecosystem include:

Visit UMD's Innovation Gateway to learn more.

The University of Maryland (UMD) is the state's flagship university driven by a community of more than 50,000 fearless Terrapins. As a leading research university and top 20 public institution, UMD is proud to be part of the Association of American Universities. Dedicated to excellence and impact for the public good, the university is propelled by a $1.4 billion joint research enterprise. UMD is the nation's first Do Good campus, and is consistently ranked for its innovation, research and top-tier academic programs. Located in the National Capital Region, the university offers an unparalleled student experience with federal internship opportunities, hundreds of academic programs and study abroad options, and top-ranked living-learning programs. Spurred by a culture of innovation and creativity, UMD's faculty are global leaders in their fields and include Nobel laureates, Pulitzer Prize winners and members of the national academies. For more information about the University of Maryland, College Park, visit umd.edu.

All the Brooks Running Shoes and Apparel on Sale Now for Black Friday

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
