Many of us started running in a Brooks trainer, and with Black Friday deals in full swing, you can stock up on must-have Brooks gear or gift that friend you’re pushing to run their first 5K.

What makes Brooks trainers so popular? Their plush, slipper-like fit and comfy cushioning put them at the center of a Venn diagram where dad shoes and serious runner trainers converge.

Several top-rated models, like the Adrenaline GTS 25, are on sale at brooksrunning.com and sites like REI and Amazon. But sizes and colors are limited, so stock up now before they’re gone for good!

We’ve also included discounted Brooks apparel, so you can run in Brooks from head to toe all winter long.

Brooks Running Sale | REI Brooks Running Sale

The stability counterpart to the Ghost 18, the Adrenaline GTS is the shoe many of us ran in when we fell in love with running. The 25th iteration is the lightest yet, with a foamy midsole, the brand’s DNA Loft v3, to absorb shock and provide soft landings. The guide rails system is Brooks’s unobtrusive stability tech for neutral runners and overpronators alike. Dense foam on the medial and lateral sides acts as bumpers to help align your knee and ankle.

“This is a fantastic shoe,” said tester Chris Garges, an overpronator and heel striker. “I’ve run in every model Adrenaline dating back to the Adrenaline 3, and this is by far the best shoe in the series. I loved everything from the fit to the ride to the cushioning.”

Brooks wanted to change what we should expect when running in a max-cushioned trainer. “We noticed that for some people, they would start running in a neutral shoe and then their body would work extra. So the internal cost was actually increasing,” said Brooks Footwear PLM and BlueLine Director Nikhil Jain.

The team set out to create a shoe that would cut that internal cost. The Glycerin Flex has a decoupled heel and Flex Zone; these components allow your foot to flex naturally instead of working against a stiff, beefy platform.

“Testing the Brooks Glycerin Flex was a novel experience for me on multiple fronts,” said Editorial Assistant Michael McDonough. “Not only was this the first Brooks running shoe I’ve ever run in, but it was also the first hyperflexible shoe that I have ever seen—and overall, it was a positive experience. The Glycerin Flex combines the benefits of a daily trainer that allows your feet to work and get stronger with the comfort and protection of a maximalist shoe.”

We like the Glycerin Max 2 for run/walking and recovery days. The shoe has 45 mm of DNA Tuned foam in the heel and a 6 mm offset. Its beefiness was off-putting for some, especially for runners hoping to get in some tempo work on their long runs. But testers said the cushioning was extremely comfy on slow days.

“I loved how comfortable the sneaker was. I instantly felt supported and it felt like I was walking on a bouncy pillow,” said wear-tester Steph Norman. “This sneaker would be great for easy/long miles where you do not have to worry about your feet hurting.”

The popular Brooks Ghost hits the trails. The shoe is outfitted for going off-road with 3 mm lugs on the TrailTack rubber outsole.

Our tester, David Hinkl, appreciated how the shoe protected his feet on gnarly surfaces. “The best part of the shoe for me was the sole,” he said. “It was beefy enough to prevent sharp rocks and pointy things from getting to my feet. Training is more fun when I don’t have to tiptoe across the trail.”

The Canopy is iconic. Despite the curtains of rain, monstrous winds, and cold temps, Des Linden was the first American woman to win the 2018 Boston Marathon after 33 years. And she wore the Canopy Jacket. This version of the Canopy (the original is also on sale with 30 percent off) can be packed into a little pouch that’s easy to store in the depths of an overpacked duffel.

The High Point keeps out the rain without overheating. Venting in the back allows airflow, while waterproof fabric and sealed inner seams keep you from getting soaked during a downpour. The jacket also packs down into a pocket.

These joggers are the softest joggers we’ve tested. They’re great for warmups, cooldowns, and traveling. I should know—I trekked Japan after running the Tokyo Marathon in these.

The Run Visible not only ensures you’re seen with reflective detail, but it also keeps you warm thanks to Thermolite insulation. Three pockets provide storage. That hi-vis orange color also helps you pop out in low light.

The Chaser is for runners who love a soft compression short with ample storage. The high-rise Chaser has side pockets for stashing your phone and leg-opening grippers to prevent them from riding up.

Grayscale Bets $1 Million on Zcash as Privacy Coin Tumbles

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Grayscale Bets $1 Million on Zcash as Privacy Coin Tumbles

Grayscale is putting money and marketing muscle behind Zcash, even as the privacy coin’s price retreats.

The asset manager said Tuesday that it plans to direct $1 million to nonprofits and initiatives that support the Zcash ecosystem. It has already awarded $100,000 each to four groups: the Zcash Foundation, policy group Pretty Good Policy for Zcash, Swiss-based Shielded Labs and Project Tachyon, a scaling effort led by one of Zcash’s co-founders.

The funding comes from fees on The Zcash ETF, which trades under the ticker ZCSH. Grayscale said it intends to reinvest all of the sponsor fees it collects from the fund, for up to its first year as an exchange-traded product, into developing and marketing the network and educating people about it.

The commitment is voluntary, and Grayscale can change or end it at any time.

Grayscale also launched Zcash Invest, an educational website and social media presence, along with a national ad campaign, “Zcash: The Next Era of Crypto,” which kicked off with a Wall Street Journal wrap. The ads will run in print, outdoor, digital, streaming and social channels, featuring a zebra motif drawn from the Zcash community.

“Financial privacy is becoming essential as more economic activity moves onchain and AI makes it easier to analyze,” said Steve Vanourny, Grayscale’s head of index.

The ETF fund listed on NYSE Arca in August, after a critical flaw in Zcash’s shielded pool sent ZEC down about 50% just three months earlier before a fix arrived in July. Grayscale later split the fund’s shares to make them more affordable for investors seeking exposure to the, at the time, red hot privacy coin.

Competition is building, too, with another Zcash ETF in the works from the Winklevoss twins and Europe’s first Zcash exchange-traded product already launched.

Grayscale’s recent push, though, lands during a rough stretch for Zcash. ZEC fell 11.3% over 24 hours to about $1,043 on Tuesday after briefly dipping below $1,000, according to CoinGecko.

It’s down roughly 19% over 30 days and well off its October highs above $1,500, though still up about 75% from a year ago. Perpetual futures open interest stands at about $2.3 billion, and Zcash previously tumbled after leverage built up during its rally.

Appear Inc. Begins Pre-Production of Solid-State Battery, Targets Mid-2027 Launch

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Field trials complete across motorcycles, e-bikes and scooters. Pre-production underway. Limited production on track for mid-2027, with full-scale production in 2028.

SAN FRANCISCO, Nov. 10, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Appear Inc., a San Francisco-based developer of next-generation solid-state battery technology, today announced that it has moved from proving its technology to producing it.

The company has completed field trials on three types of electric vehicle: motorcycles, e-bikes and scooters. It has also completed advanced manufacturing trials and started pre-production manufacturing with a manufacturing partner.

Alongside this technical progress, Appear has added new distributors and strategic partners in selective established and emerging markets. The company remains on track to begin limited commercial production in mid-2027, followed by full-scale production in 2028.

Most electric vehicles today run on lithium-ion batteries, which use a liquid electrolyte to move charge between the electrodes. Solid-state batteries replace that liquid with a solid material. This is widely seen as a path to safer batteries, with no flammable liquid to leak or ignite, and to more energy in a smaller, lighter cell.

Watch the video: https://youtu.be/W2H44vFPjXY

Appear's solid-state technology is built around three priorities: safety, performance and manufacturability. For motorcycles, e-bikes and scooters, where weight, range and safety matter on every ride, that combination is especially valuable.

Following its advanced manufacturing trials, Appear has moved into pre-production with its manufacturing partner and is refining its processes for volume manufacturing.

New distributors and strategic partners in selective established and emerging markets are expected to give Appear regional market access and distribution channels at launch. Partner details remain confidential.

"We are moving beyond development and validation and into commercialization," said Prashant Rurs, Founder & CEO of Appear Inc. "We remain on track to begin limited production in mid-2027 and to scale up to full production in 2028."

Appear Inc. is a San Francisco-based company developing next-generation solid-state battery technology for electric mobility and other energy storage applications.

Apple has new Mac app coming to make running local AI models easy

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Leaker Aaron Perris has discovered evidence of a new Mac app in the works at Apple called Harbor, which makes it easy to run local AI models across one or more Macs.

The Mac has been getting a lot more popular this year, both because of budget-friendly options like MacBook Neo but also as a tool for running AI models locally on Mac Studio, Mac mini, and more.

It seems Apple plans to lean harder into the latter use case, as leaker Aaron Perris has discovered a new Mac app called Harbor that’s been designed to turn your Mac into a private AI inference server.

Here’s Perris’s post on X with the details:

Apple is working on a new app called HarborHarbor turns your Mac into a private AI inference server. Run open-weight models locally on Apple silicon—your data never leaves your hardware, and nothing reaches the cloud. Connect multiple Mac computers over Thunderbolt 5 to pool… pic.twitter.com/UYJBQeX4Nf

As the post notes, here’s what the app does:

Harbor turns your Mac into a private AI inference server. Run open-weight models locally on Apple silicon—your data never leaves your hardware, and nothing reaches the cloud. Connect multiple Mac computers over Thunderbolt 5 to pool unified memory, enabling large models and higher concurrency that a single computer can handle alone.

Harbor is currently in a private preview under Apple’s Claris brand, but it seems likely that we’ll see it launch to the public in the weeks and months ahead.

I want an uglier GTA 6 that runs at 60 FPS – 30 FPS on console is more reason to wait for PC

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Opinion | Please, Rockstar, join us in the light of 60 FPS

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GTA 6 is targeting 30 FPS on PS5 and Xbox Series X. The same goes for the beefier PS5 Pro, which can now sit another exclusive on the shelf alongside Sony's marketing deal for the game: ray-traced shadows that have seen some people shell out $1,300. This is all fine and expected news, yet I'm saddled by the same tiny disappointment I feel every time a big console release targets 30 FPS.

It's not that I'll turn my nose at any game that isn't running at 60 FPS or higher, filthy PC gamer that I am. But if we're talking preferences, yeah, I do wish everything ran at 60 FPS at 1440p or below. I'd gladly accept lower graphical fidelity as a tradeoff for that and, more pertinently, I'll happily wait to play GTA 6 on PC if it means better performance.

This is easy for me to say, of course. I didn't have to move mountains to get a game of this scale running at 30 FPS to begin with. It might sound easy to put fidelity and frame rate on a scale and just subtract material until the FPS side rises, but I know it's not that simple, even if cuts could surely be made.

It's not just about the visible sweat, intricate clothing, innumerable environmental wrinkles, granular animations, or ray-traced shadows – all the little details that people at Rockstar have presumably achieved through blood sacrifice. This might actually be the best-looking game ever made by many metrics. But GTA 6 also has to handle dense NPC crowds, a sprawling city, seamless character switching, and all the unseen systems supporting that experience. That, to me, is the truly important, gameplay-affecting stuff.

I never care about graphics in any way that matters, and I always care about smoothness

It all amounts to more things for that frame rate to haul, and Rockstar seemingly chose 30 FPS with all the fixins' over higher FPS with all the sacrifices. I understand and respect that. Devs should make what they want, as ludicrously complex as they want it to be. But on that scale, my taste leans heavily toward frame rate for a few reasons.

Console generational leaps are getting smaller. This generation has, as far as I'm concerned, had only two real difference-makers: M.2 NVMe SSDs that dramatically improve loading times, and the spread of performance-focused graphic settings (or performance modes). As you may have guessed, I go for performance mode every single time.

Some games benefit more from having higher frame rates than others, but maximum FPS is always my pick until I hit 60. A fast-paced martial arts game like Phantom Blade Zero would be seriously hurt by a low or inconsistent frame rate, for instance. But I'm currently playing Persona 3 Reload, and its turn-based combat and simple environments aren't really bothered as long as the frame rate is half-decent and stable. But the choice is still simple: I never care about graphics in any way that matters, and I always care about smoothness. GTA 6 isn't a frenetic wuxia fiesta, but its driving and shooting definitely push it toward the more FPS-sensitive camp in my mind.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

There's a point where I lose interest in graphical improvements, and that point came at least 10 years ago. I can appreciate a cutting-edge game, whether it's GTA 6 NPCs whose calves flex with each step or a huge RPG like Cyberpunk 2077 with every setting cranked to the moon. But if AAA games eternally looked like late-gen PS4 releases, I honestly wouldn't complain. My threshold for game that looks good is comfortably below that, and an art style far removed from realism is more likely to grab me anyway. Now and forever, I'm more interested in games that run better and console gaming that feels, not necessarily looks, more like my PC experience (again, M.2s really helped with this). Hell, right now I just want consoles that people can actually afford, and pushing hardware to fruitlessly advance graphics won't get us those.

Every generation, I think these will surely be the systems to normalize 60 FPS, and I'm consistently let down by games going in the opposite direction for visuals that don't, and will never, move me. In all graphical discussions, my mind goes to an early Uncharted: Drake's Fortune demo showing how water would convincingly dampen Nathan Drake's clothes and then dry in the sun. Nearly 20 years later, it feels like we are still hung up on the equivalent of Nathan Drake getting wet even though this detail didn't mean squat to the experience of Uncharted and this kind of thing is infinitely less novel and relevant today. The real or imagined title of 'best-looking game ever made' has never meant less, and a significantly uglier version of GTA 6 would still assuredly blow my socks off. I'll tell you what would really impress me: a promise that this runs at twice the frame rate of GTA 5, a 13-year-old game.

Austin has been a game journalist for 12 years, having freelanced for the likes of PC Gamer, Eurogamer, IGN, Sports Illustrated, and more while finishing his journalism degree. He's been with GamesRadar+ since 2019. They've yet to realize his position is a cover for his career-spanning Destiny column, and he's kept the ruse going with a lot of news and the occasional feature, all while playing as many roguelikes as possible.

InfoQ Homepage News Cloudflare Makes Basin Serverless Analytics Generally Available

Nov 10, 2026 2 min read

Cloudflare has announced the general availability of Basin, a serverless analytics platform built on Apache Iceberg and R2 Object Storage. The managed service supports open data formats and integrates with tools such as DuckDB, Snowflake, and Apache Spark, with no data egress fees.

Introduced in preview as "Cloudflare Data Platform" just over a year ago, Basin combines data ingestion and SQL-based transformation, managed table maintenance, and distributed SQL queries, allowing developers to analyze large datasets without managing infrastructure. Marc Selwan, senior product manager at Cloudflare, and Micah Wylde, principal engineer at Cloudflare, explain why eliminating egress costs is central to the design:

A large driving force in the development and adoption of Basin is the continued growth we are seeing in the open Apache Iceberg ecosystem. (...) With Basin, you can read and write your data using any Iceberg-compatible engine, including PyIceberg, DuckDB, Snowflake, and Apache Spark. That kind of data portability is only possible with free egress, which allows developers to access their data in Cloudflare from the wide variety of tools in the ecosystem, regardless of region or cloud.

The platform combines three services: Basin Pipelines, formerly Cloudflare Pipelines, ingests events from Workers, HTTP endpoints, and Logpush, applies SQL transformations, and writes data to Apache Iceberg tables or R2; Basin Catalog manages Iceberg metadata and automates table maintenance; and Basin SQL provides a serverless, distributed SQL engine for querying Iceberg tables.

Basin aims to make analytics more accessible to smaller teams by running data ingestion, preparation, and queries on Cloudflare’s global network. This reduces the need to manage server clusters or move data between services, lowering the operational overhead of building and maintaining analytics pipelines. Selwan and Wylde explain the renaming:

Why Basin? A basin is where rivers from many sources come together to a single point. We felt that Basin perfectly captures how the platform is used: Pipelines brings data into Basin Catalog while Basin SQL makes it instantly queryable.

While some practitioners describe Basin as "open analytics infrastructure without the warehouse cathedral," Ramin Muradov, lead data analyst at PashaPay , comments:

I don't think it will knock Snowflake off its spot, at least not anytime soon. Cloudflare itself says Basin isn't a replacement for every warehouse workload. But there's a big layer of the market made up of teams whose data is too small to justify dedicated infrastructure and too big to fit in Excel. They now have an option.

Cloudflare is not the only cloud provider targeting unified analytics: as separately reported on InfoQ, AWS recently acquired DuckLabs and integrated DuckDB into Aurora PostgreSQL, aiming to simplify analytics across transactional and object storage data, while Google repositioned over the years BigQuery as the "single, unified AI-ready data platform."

Since its preview, Basin has increased ingestion capacity to 3 GB/s per stream, expanded SQL capabilities, improved Iceberg table maintenance, and added Logpush integration and Terraform support. Planned features, with no committed release dates, include real-time processing capabilities, support for the latest Iceberg specification, adaptive table-maintenance, and additional controls for data sovereignty and access management.

Cloudflare's Basin tutorial explains how to ingest events with Basin Pipelines, store them in Iceberg tables managed by Basin Catalog, and query them using Basin SQL.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog
